In the framework of an exact analytical Kramers--Wannier transfer-matrix approach and using an extension of the combinatorial Vdovichenko--Feynman method, we have obtained exact solution for Helmholtz free energy and consequent expression for the principal eigenvalue of the Kramers--Wannier transfer-matrix. In addition, we derived the exact expression for spontaneous magnetization. The usage of both expressions made it possible to perform the comprehensive studies of thermodynamic and magnetic properties of the Calandre lattice itself and a wide variety of its affiliated lattices. Among them, diversified interrelations between Calandre lattice and already known and even some novel lattices. These lattices have also revealed such effects as magnetic frustrations, multiple phase transitions and magnetic reentrancy.

### Paper [52]
Title: A Geometry-Guided Feature-Space Framework for Interpretable Unsupervised 4D-STEM Analysis of Complex Multiphase Materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10853
Authors: Arda Genc, Daniel A. Bolden, Albert Nguyen, Daniel S. Gianola, Ravit Silverstein
Abstract: Rapid, high-fidelity analysis of four-dimensional scanning transmission electron microscopy (4D-STEM) datasets from heterogeneous materials remains challenging because coexisting phases, orientation variants, local strain gradients, and intrinsic TEM thin-foil effects produce complex, overlapping diffraction signatures. Here, we present a machine-learning (ML) framework that combines physics-informed geometric descriptors with weighted high-dimensional embeddings to identify latent structural patterns with minimal prior knowledge. By constructing a regularized descriptor space before embedding and density-based clustering, the method improves cluster stability, suppresses noise-driven fragmentation, and reduces sensitivity to embedding hyperparameters. Spatial weighting produced the largest stability gain, increasing median cluster persistence from 0.04 to 0.55 while reducing the noise fraction from approximately 22% to 0%. Rotation weighting increased DBCV from 0.80 to 0.92, although the noise fraction rose from 0.2% to 1.0%, highlighting a trade-off between improved density separation and noise rejection. We validate the framework on two benchmark systems: Si/Si$_x$Ge$_{1-x}$ epitaxial layers with continuous lattice variation and deformation, and multiphase Ti-Nb alloys containing distinct phases and orientation variants. Comparisons with a pretrained vanilla autoencoder and model-free pattern vectorization show that the physics-informed geometric descriptor provides higher cluster fidelity, greater interpretability, and substantially lower dimensionality.

### Paper [53]
Title: Multilayer Heater Nano cryotrons: Electrothermal Modeling of Geometry-Dependent Switching
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10868
Authors: Baibhari Priya Barua, Md Rahatul Islam Udoy, Shamiul Alam, Md Shafayat Hossain, Ahmedullah Aziz
Abstract: This paper presents a finite-element framework to study how device geometry affects the switching current of multilayer heater nano cryotrons (hTrons). The device comprises a 50 nm Au/5 nm Ti heater, a 100 nm SiO2 spacer, a 23.6 nm NbN channel, and a Si substrate. Two-dimensional electrothermal simulations examine nine heater and channel width combinations using widths of 100, 500, and 1000 nm. Three-dimensional simulations show how heat flows through the layers and spreads across the channel near the heater-channel overlap. Heater electrical losses and heat transport are coupled, whereas the channel switching threshold is obtained separately from a temperature-dependent constitutive relation. Published global temperature measurements support the switching-law calibration, and heater-current measurements assess the resulting device response. The model measurement comparisons show a systematic reduction of switching-current density with heating, but also geometry-dependent discrepancies in the low-drive plateau and transition shape. A common extraction protocol is formulated for heater-current and heater-power requirements at a specified suppression level, while separating fitted device amplitudes from controlled geometry effects. The resulting comparisons connect thermal fields to design metrics while distinguishing calibration from validation and excluding transient-performance claims from the stationary analysis

### Paper [54]
Title: A data-driven approach for systematic correction of electronic bandgaps
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10889
Authors: Pamela C. Carvalho, Asa Marif, Erna K. Delczeg-Czirjak, Sergei Simak, Utkarsh Singh, Olle Eriksson
Abstract: We demonstrate that a single machine-learning correction, applied to bandgaps from density functional theory calculations with two of the most frequently used energy functionals, results in bandgaps that are close to the experimental values. This solves a classical problem of computationally efficient electronic structure methods, to give reliable bandgaps that can be compared to experiments. The employed approach relies on materials specific feature engineering of machine learning models, and is shown to have close accuracy to that of much more expensive calculations based on the GW-approximation or hybrid functionals. The findings reported here open the possibility to use efficient calculations in materials design workflows that need reliable bandgaps, with implications for investigations targeting large computational databases or for investigations that are entirely predictive in nature.

### Paper [55]
Title: Three-dimensional multipole states of the Bose-Einstein condensate of polar molecules in a shallow two-dimensional trapping potential
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10896
Authors: S. Sanjay, S. Saravana Veni, Boris A. Malomed
Abstract: We investigate the formation, stability, and dynamics of three-dimensional (3D) elongated multipole quantum droplets (QDs) in a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) of polar molecules with long-range interactions between molecular electric dipoles. The condensate is confined by a 2D trapping potential, which is a combination of the harmonic-oscillator potential and a central Gaussian potential hill. The system is modeled by an extended Gross-Pitaevskii (GP) equation, incorporating the dipole-dipole interactions and beyond-mean-field Lee-Huang-Yang (LHY) correction, which play a crucial role in stabilizing self-bound QD structures. In regimes of weak and strong confinement, stable multipole states with the number of lobes $n=2S$ are created by means of the imaginary-time-propagation (ITP) method from inputs with vorticity $S\leq 2$. The so created multipole QDs carry no angular momentum. Stable higher-order multipole states can be produced by the ITP method from inputs with $S\geq 3$ up to $S=6$ (i.e., $2S=12$ lobes) only under strong confinement and when the molecule number $N$ exceeds a threshold value. We analyze the dependence of the chemical potential, energy per particle, and peak density on $N$ and $S$ in the weak- and strong-confinement regimes. A noteworthy fact is that, below another threshold value of $N$, the minimum-energy (ground) state is created by the input with $S=1$, rather than $S=0$. Simulations of perturbed evolution reveal that, despite satisfying energetic stability criteria, only a subset of the multipole QDs are truly stable, with higher-order ones tending to develop instabilities or relax toward lower-order states. These results highlight the essential roles of the trapping potential, anisotropy of the dipole-dipole interactions, and LHY-induced repulsion in the formation and structural stability of multipole QDs.

### Paper [56]
Title: Non-reciprocal Rouse chain model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10904
Authors: Apaza Leonardo, Sandoval Mario
Abstract: We study the center-of-mass mean-squared displacement (MSD) of a free non-reciprocal Rouse chain. Three stochastic models are considered: an overdamped linear chain, an underdamped linear chain, and an underdamped nonlinear chain with finite natural bond length that fully takes into account the elastic interactions. For the overdamped model, an exact transient expression for the center-of-mass MSD is obtained from the associated Ornstein--Uhlenbeck process. We show that non-reciprocity enhances the effective diffusion with respect to the classic Rouse model, and that it separates the translational right and left zero modes, so the arithmetic center of mass is not, in general, the dynamically decoupled translational coordinate. It is found that the normalized left zero mode gives a closed expression for the effective diffusivity in terms of the non-reciprocal parameter and the number of beads ($N$) which still scales as $N^{-1}$. Interestingly, the same long-time diffusivity coefficient is found for both, the underdamped linear model and for the less studied finite-natural-length nonlinear model. It is concluded that inertia and finite bond length, modify the transient dynamics of the system, while non-reciprocity determines the long-time diffusion coefficient.

### Paper [57]
Title: 2D coherent spectroscopy of topological insulators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10905
Authors: Tha\'{i}s V. Trevisan, Micha{\l} Papaj
Abstract: Multidimensional coherent spectroscopy probes the response of a system to a sequence of time-separated light pulses, and has attracted significant interest in recent years. In the frequency space, it generates multidimensional spectra, in which information about different degrees of freedom of the system is generically disentangled. In this work, we develop a quantum geometric formulation of multidimensional optical susceptibilities in insulating systems. Focusing on a two-pulse setup and working in the velocity gauge, we derive analytical expressions for second-order and third-order two-dimensional coherent spectra (2DCS). We focus on the third-order ABB response, which remains finite in centrosymmetric systems where the second-order susceptibility vanishes by inversion symmetry. Its paramagnetic contribution supports an echo signal that probes products of four interband Berry-connection matrix elements, which reduce in two-band systems to bilinear combinations of the quantum geometric tensor of the occupied band. Using continuum and square-lattice models of two-dimensional Chern insulators, we show how the echo resolves the momentum-space sign structure of these geometric quantities. For the $xxyx$ response of the models considered, the number of nodes in the echo signal is related to the band topology. Our results extend the quantum-geometric interpretation of multidimensional spectroscopy to higher-order responses, showing that third-order 2DCS provides access to geometric structures beyond those probed at lower orders.

### Paper [58]
Title: Lifting mechanical zero modes without dispersion
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10937
Authors: Joohan Nam
Abstract: Floppy frameworks carry flat bands of zero-frequency mechanisms with no finite-frequency resonance, and couplings that lift them usually spread them. We show that an on-site tensor lifts a mechanism band without first-order dispersion exactly when, projected onto the mechanisms, it acts as wavevector-independent scalars on orthogonal pieces. Three structures achieve this: local normal modes, invariant symmetry sectors, and a balance law. A mechanism moves both ends of every bond between two vertex classes equally along it, and where the inter-class bond tensors are class scalars this balance law fixes its norm shares and hence the flat value from geometry alone. For the fluorite net the band lies outside the tensor spectrum, at 14/9 of the contact stiffness to first order, and stays flat to all orders in a fixed cage. A placement inside a tiling dual that keeps the common symmetry supplies such tensors, because each contact star inherits its site symmetry. Ten catalogued dual pairs lift flat; half are eigenvalue cases a resolved count matches, and the balance law explains flat bands in random cages too. Duality makes the rigid cage common. In a printable beam-frame lattice, contacts that lock joint rotation remove first-order bending dispersion whenever bonded mechanisms are orthogonal local normals.

### Paper [59]
Title: In-Band Boundary Resonances from Coalescing Zero-Group-Velocity Points
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10939
Authors: Amir Rajabpoor Alisepahi, Charles A. Downing, Jihong Ma
Abstract: Boundary states generally reside in band gaps, whereas in-band confinement typically requires exact decoupling from propagating modes. We demonstrate an alternative mechanism in reciprocal, topologically trivial Hermitian lattices arising from the coalescence of two zero-group-velocity (ZGV) points. At coalescence, the group velocity and inverse effective mass vanish simultaneously, and the corresponding Bloch root acquires higher multiplicity. The coalesced root remains propagating, whereas nearby root splitting yields weakly evanescent channels that, together with a bulk-matched termination, generate a boundary-weighted resonance within the propagating spectrum. The mechanism is realized analytically in a two-leg ladder model through the coalescence of time-reversal-related ZGVs and extends to distinct ZGVs produced by longer-range interactions. Finite-size scaling distinguishes the resulting resonance from extended and defect-bound states. Scanning laser Doppler vibrometry on a 3D-printed elastic ladder reveals boundary enhancement in a nearly flat band region, consistent with operation slightly below criticality. ZGV coalescence thus provides a bulk framework for in-band boundary resonances in reciprocal Hermitian lattices.

### Paper [60]
Title: Deintercalation Equilibria Control the Composition, Structure, and Electronic Quality in the Synthesis of 2$M$-WS$_2$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10941
Authors: Brenna C. Bierman, Taeran Yang, Jon Ramos, Sara Yarworth, James Lazarcik, Daniel A. Rhodes
Abstract: Alkali-metal intercalation-deintercalation (AMID) enables access to metastable layered materials such as 2$M$-WS$_2$. Mechanistic studies of AMID have focused on intercalation; how deintercalation proceeds and influences crystal properties is comparatively poorly understood. Here, we assess 34 deintercalation treatments of 2$M$-K$_x$WS$_2$ and find that K$^+$ removal proceeds through coupled redox and ion-exchange equilibria, whose positions set the composition, structure, and electronic quality of the products. Across a series of neat solvents, K$^+$ deintercalation amount increased with solvent polarity and was driven to near-completion upon addition of nonoxidizing crown ether chelators, regardless of solvent polarity. Spontaneous reintercalation occurred when 2$M$-WS$_2$ was placed in dilute aqueous solutions of the strong base KOH but not the neutral salt KCl. These results suggest that the extent of deintercalation is governed by the stability of K$^+$ in solution, rather than oxidant strength alone, consistent with a reaction under thermodynamic control. Treatments that bias the deintercalation equilibrium most strongly toward the products while avoiding deleterious side reactions produce the highest quality 2$M$-WS$_2$. A six-hour treatment with acidified K$_2$Cr$_2$O$_7$ gave the highest average residual resistivity ratio ($\overline{RRR}$). Residual K$^+$, quantified by ICP-MS, was found in all treated samples and appeared to impose an upper bound on $\overline{RRR}$. Treatments yielding crystals with $\overline{RRR}$ below this bound did not always afford 2$M$-WS$_2$; we observed two new structures in bleach-treated samples and signatures of alternative phases under other conditions. This work demonstrates that deintercalation is not merely a processing step in AMID but a mechanistically rich reaction that might be rationally tuned to improve and control metastable materials.

### Paper [61]
Title: Imaging spin entanglement with a scanning tunneling microscope
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10946
Authors: J. C. G. Henriques, B. Vi\~na-Baus\'a, I. Brihuega, J. Fern\'andez-Rossier
Abstract: Artificial spin arrays assembled on surfaces enable atomic-scale studies of quantum correlations. Here we establish a hierarchy of connections between spin inelastic electron tunneling spectroscopy (IETS) and entanglement in spin-$1/2$ systems. We first show that the number of spectroscopically active transitions in a spin-$1/2$ dimer serves as an entanglement witness. Using the leading-order tunneling conductance for the tip placed on spin $i$, $dI(i)/dV$, we then relate spectral moments $\mathcal{M}^{(n)}(i)=\int V^n(d^2I(i)/dV^2)\,dV$ to quantum correlations. The zeroth moment probes the one-tangle in systems with a gapped, nondegenerate ground state. For rotationally invariant Heisenberg systems, the first moment yields thermal spin correlators $\langle\boldsymbol S(i)\cdot\boldsymbol S(j)\rangle$, enabling reconstruction of nearest-neighbor concurrence and spatial imaging of entanglement in spin chains. It also provides access to the internal energy and magnetic specific heat, both usable as entanglement witnesses. Applying this approach to experimental IETS data from nanographene spin chains, we find good agreement with theory. We further discuss how the second moment probes longer-range correlations and the inverse moment relates to local spin susceptibility. For spin-$1/2$ trimers, we connect inter-multiplet inelastic spectral weight to genuine tripartite entanglement quantified by the concurrence fill. Finally, we reconcile the local character of IETS with its sensitivity to nonlocal entanglement through an analogy with digital quantum circuits, where Hamiltonian evolution implements multiqubit operations. These results establish IETS as a tool for quantifying and imaging entanglement in engineered on-surface spin systems.

### Paper [62]
Title: Superconductivity and Polar Metallicity in Atomically Thin Nitride Superlattices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10976
Authors: Huma Nawaz, Dawei Wang, C. S. Ting, Laurent Bellaiche
Abstract: The integration of polar and superconducting materials in the atomically thin limit typically results in a competition that suppresses the superconducting order. Here, we challenge this conventional view by employing first-principles calculations to investigate 1:1 superlattices composed of alternating atomically thin layers of TiN/AlN and {\delta}-NbN/AlN. Contrary to the expectation that dense insulating barriers would quench superconductivity, we find that the polar interface can coexist with a superconducting state. We observe that the transition temperature (Tc) of TiN is enhanced by nearly 2 K over its bulk value, while {\delta}-NbN maintains a relatively high Tc exceeding 20 K. We demonstrate that this resilience arises from the formation of a polar metal, where the strong internal electric fields of the AlN layers are partially screened by a charge redistribution within the nitride layers. This screening mechanism, along with the geometric confinement, creates a quasi-two-dimensional electronic environment, establishing these superlattices as a novel platform for exploring the coexistence of structural inversion-symmetry breaking and superconductivity in the two-dimensional limit.

### Paper [63]
Title: Efficient Plane-to-Point Energy Transfer from Supramolecular Excitonic Systems to Molecular Acceptors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11000
Authors: Dimuthu H. Thanippuli Arachchi (Massachusetts Institute of Technology), Oliver M. Nix (Massachusetts Institute of Technology), Jianshu Cao (Massachusetts Institute of Technology), Moungi G. Bawendi (Massachusetts Institute of Technology)
Abstract: Collective excitonic interactions in supramolecular assemblies offer opportunities for efficient light harvesting and directed energy transport. Here, we demonstrate efficient energy transfer from two-dimensional silica-encapsulated TDBC J-aggregate sheets to covalently attached ATTO 590 molecular acceptors. Silica encapsulation preserves the J-aggregate excitonic properties while enabling acceptor functionalization at a defined separation from the donor plane. Steady-state donor quenching and acceptor sensitization yield energy-transfer efficiencies of 75-81% in solution, while time-resolved photoluminescence measurements reveal donor lifetime shortening. We develop a generalized Forster resonance energy-transfer model for an extended planar donor coupled to localized molecular acceptors, predicting an inverse fourth-power dependence of the transfer rate on donor-acceptor separation. Using experimental spectroscopic data and estimated structural parameters, the model predicts an energy-transfer rate of 4.77 x 10^10 s^-1, approximately ten times faster than the intrinsic donor decay rate, corresponding to a theoretical energy-transfer efficiency of approximately 91%. These findings provide a framework for developing hybrid excitonic architectures for light harvesting.

### Paper [64]
Title: Learning Electronic Band Structures Across the s, p, d Blocks with an Empirical Pseudopotential Network
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11070
Authors: Qiang Zhu
Abstract: Electronic band structures govern how crystals conduct electrons and absorb light. Density-functional theory (DFT) computes them through costly self-consistent iterations, while existing machine-learning models either predict isolated scalar properties or output Hamiltonians that still require DFT solvers. Here we present EpNet, a learned empirical pseudopotential for crystals spanning 63 elements across the \textit{s}, \textit{p} and \textit{d} blocks. An equivariant neural network builds its Hamiltonian from physics-motivated local potentials and nonlocal projectors. Trained solely on band energies, EpNet predicts complete band structures directly from atomic coordinates. On unseen chemical systems, its median errors are \winAll\ eV for band energies and \gapAll\ eV for band gaps. On 600,000 structures from an independent database, its band-gap errors are overall less than half those of existing models, at about 1\% of DFT cost. EpNet thus enables fast screening of electronic properties for materials discovery and electronic-structure simulations of large systems.

### Paper [65]
Title: Spin Conductivity: Insight From Perturbation Theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11077
Authors: Xu Chen, Mingbo Dou, Yu Sui, Xianjie Wang, L. L. Tao
Abstract: The investigation on the spin conductivity has now been blooming in burgeoning altermagnetic materials. Here, an alternative and elementary derivation for the spin conductivity expression is presented. This is done within the context of perturbation theory. It indicates that our derived expression for the $\mathcal{T}$-even ($\mathcal{T}$ for time reversal) spin conductivity agrees exactly with those derived from the Kubo linear response theory and quantum kinetic theory. In addition, the expression for the $\mathcal{T}$-odd spin conductivity in the clean limit is exactly consistent with those presented previously. We show that the $\mathcal{T}$-odd ($\mathcal{T}$-even) spin conductivity arises from the contribution of zeroth-order (first-order) wave function correction. Our work provides some physical insight into the spin conductivity from the perturbation theory and shines more light on the charge-to-spin conversion mechanism in magnetic materials.

### Paper [66]
Title: Odd Darcy Law: Odd Viscosity and Edge Currents in Porous Media
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11138
Authors: Raphael Maire, Ignacio Pagonabarraga
Abstract: We derive and numerically validate an odd Darcy law for chiral active fluids in porous media, in which an antisymmetric permeability tensor induces a flow transverse to the applied pressure gradient. We identify two distinct mechanisms leading to this odd response: odd viscosity in the fluid or the nonlinear coupling of edge currents to inertia. We then show how the transverse transport depends on porosity, boundary friction, and chirality.

### Paper [67]
Title: Coherent Conversion of an Interacting Dark-State-Polariton Condensate into a Photonic Bose-Einstein Condensate
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11180
Authors: G. D. de Moraes Neto, M. A. de Ponte, N. G. de Almeida, Miled H. Y. Moussa
Abstract: An interacting condensate can retain an observable imprint of its preparation after the interactions are switched off. For light, accessing this regime requires separating the formation of a photon condensate from the material medium that redistributes its energy. We investigate a multimode-cavity scheme in which an interacting dark-state-polariton condensate is coherently converted into a confined photonic Bose-Einstein condensate as the atom-cavity coupling is removed. The polariton mass, matter fraction, and particle number control the condensate occupation during preparation. Remarkably, changing the mass also modifies the condensate's response to repulsion through the spatial overlap of its orbitals, even when the entire single-particle spectrum is held fixed. Ideal mode-preserving conversion conserves the full natural-orbital occupation spectrum, while finite-time few-body calculations show that additional occupation changes during transfer are much smaller than those established during interacting preparation. Once decoupled from matter, the photons evolve under the free cavity Hamiltonian while retaining correlations acquired in the interacting gas. Interference between cavity modes produces oscillations in the photon density, while resonant pair correlations persist after interaction removal and distinguish the resulting photon state from a quadratic thermal state with the same one-body density matrix.

### Paper [68]
Title: Electron cooling mediated by transverse optical phonons in doped SrTiO$_3$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11225
Authors: A. A. Zyuzin
Abstract: Soft transverse optical (TO) phonons in doped SrTiO$_3$ couple to electrons through the polarization field, quadratically in the paraelectric phase and, additionally, linearly in the polar phase due to the onset of spontaneous polarization. We investigate heat flow between a three-dimensional electron gas and soft TO phonons. For two-TO-phonon scattering, the heat flow of hot electrons crosses over from $T^5$ to $T^3$ at the Bloch-Gruneisen temperature. In the polar phase, an additional one-TO-phonon channel emerges, yielding a softer temperature dependence of the heat flow, $T^3$ at low and $T$ at high temperatures, with the spectral weight set by the spontaneous polarization. This channel may dominate at low temperatures and leads to a nonmonotonic temperature dependence of the heat flow in the vicinity of the polar transition.

### Paper [69]
Title: The Outer Eshelby Problem in Amorphous Solids with Odd Dipole Screening
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11251
Authors: Avanish Kumar, Jin Shang, Itamar Procaccia
Abstract: The so-called "2-dimensional Eshelby problem" was defined by JD Eshelby, where a circle is cut out of an elastic sheet, deformed into an ellipse, and forced back into the material. This creates a quadrupolar response which turned out to be very relevant for describing plastic deformations in amorphous solids. Moreover, the resulting strain field has been used to describe how elasto-plastic models respond to external strains. It is thus important to examine carefully the Eshelby problem in amorphous solids, especially when anomalous elasticity is at play. Here we solve the Eshelby problem in amorphous solids with both even and odd dipole screening and a complete analytical result is obtained for the displacement and strain fields. The analytical results are crucial for understanding the anomalous responses in displacement fields measured directly from experiments and numerical simulations. The computation of strain fields clearly demonstrates the deviations from their classical counterparts for perfectly elastic solids. The decomposition of the displacement field shows the excitation of higher-order Fourier modes. The presence of higher excitations other than the imposed Eshelby geometry therefore suggests the possible nonlinear mode coupling that can influence and modify the anomalous responses.

### Paper [70]
Title: Effect of Correlation on the Pressure-Induced Insulator to Metal Transition in Complex Spin-Orbit Coupled Double Perovskite La$_2$CoIr$_{1-x}$Ti$_x$O$_6$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11253
Authors: Vineeta Yadav, Sromona Nandi, Rudra Sekhar Manna
Abstract: We study the effect of electron correlation ($U$) and hydrostatic pressure on the electronic properties of double perovskites La$_2$CoIr$_{1-x}$Ti$_x$O$_6$ for $x$ = 0, 0.5, and 1, using first-principles electronic structure calculations within density functional theory (DFT). At ambient pressure, we systematically vary the electron correlation, i.e., on-site Coulomb interaction to study its effect on band gap and structural parameters. Increasing the value of $U$ enhances the band gap of all three compositions while producing only a slight variation in structural parameters. The effect of hydrostatic pressure is also studied for the same compositions. The estimated band gap values at ambient pressure closes at approximately 39, 42, and 53 GPa for $x$ = 0 (for $U_\mathrm{Co}$ = 5 eV and $U_\mathrm{Ir}$ = 2 eV), 0.5 (for $U_\mathrm{Co}$ = 5 eV and $U_\mathrm{Ir}$ = 2 eV), and 1 (for $U_\mathrm{Co}$ = 4 eV), respectively, indicating pressure-induced insulator-to-metal transitions. These results show that electron correlation stabilises the insulating state, whereas hydrostatic pressure favours the metallic state by closing the band gap. These findings highlight the tunability of correlated oxides through electron correlations and pressure, offering insights for materials design.

### Paper [71]
Title: Engineering ferroelectric nanocapacitors through interfacial phonon bias
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11256
Authors: I\~nigo Robredo-Magro, Urmimala Dey, Suehyun park, Karam Cho, Raseong Kim, Jorge \'I\~niguez-Gonz\'alez
Abstract: As ferroelectric devices approach the nanometer scale, their behavior becomes increasingly sensitive to boundary conditions. In perovskite oxide nanocapacitors, epitaxial strain and electrostatic screening are well-established control parameters. Then, while expected to contribute to the epitaxial stabilization of ferroelectric ultrathin films, the lattice-dynamical state of the electrodes has not been isolated as an independent tuning knob. Here we call this poorly explored coupling interfacial phonon bias, whereby the electrodes' phonon energy landscape reshapes that of the adjacent ferroelectric across their shared interface. Using first-principles-accurate machine-learned interatomic potentials and molecular-dynamics simulations, we examine this mechanism in symmetric BiFeO${}_3$ nanocapacitors in contact with a metallic perovskite oxide. By independently controlling the orientatio and amplitude of the electrodes' octahedral-tilt landscape, while keeping conventional elastic and electric boundary conditions fixed, we show that the electrodes' phonon energy landscape can modify the stability of competing BiFeO${}_3$ phases. In particular, this allows us to shift the ferroelectric transition temperature and even induce antiferroelectric-like switching at ambient conditions. We thus showcase interfacial phonon bias as a complementary and powerful strategy to modify and optimize the properties of ultrathin epitaxial films.

### Paper [72]
Title: Theory of spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy with topological insulator tips
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11267
Authors: Zhuo-Yuan Shi, Chuang Li, Lin Jiao, Huiqiu Yuan, Zhenyu Wang, Xianhui Chen, Lun-Hui Hu
Abstract: Topological insulators have enabled a new class of spin-sensitive scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) probes based on helical boundary states. However, how these probes generate a net spin-polarized tunneling current remains unclear. We present a theory of spin-polarized STM with topological insulator tips and show that asymmetric tunneling-contact geometry and spin dephasing enable helical boundary states to generate a spin-polarized tunneling current. Within the standard Landauer-B\"uttiker formalism, we demonstrate this mechanism for a quantum spin Hall (QSH) insulator tip: asymmetric contacts make the two counter-propagating helical edge states, which carry opposite spins, traverse path lengths that differ by $\Delta L$, and spin dephasing induces backscattering that reduces the conductance more strongly for the longer path. For a representative asymmetric contact and weak spin dephasing, the QSH tip exhibits a tunneling spin polarization of $P_s=-17.2\%$ under positive bias, whose magnitude can reach $50\%$ for larger $\Delta L$, and which remains robust against Rashba spin-orbit coupling. Crucially, this polarization, dominated by helical edge transport rather than by bulk states, naturally reverses sign with the bias polarity owing to spin-momentum locking. Our findings are consistent with recent STM experiments using topological tips.

### Paper [73]
Title: Parametrizations of the solutions of Love equations and their momenta
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11268
Authors: Marco Faccioli, Andrea Colcelli, Danny Laghi, Yu Zhou, Giacomo Gori, Andrea Trombettoni
Abstract: The Love equation is a paradigmatic example of a Fredholm integral equation of great interest in mathematics and physics, where its applications range from electrostatic problems to exactly solvable models for one-dimensional systems. In this work we propose a method to approximate the normalized solutions of Love equations of the first and second kind via a parametrization obtained by Hermite interpolation at Chebyshev nodes. Following the same approach, we also obtain approximations for the second, fourth and sixth momenta of the solution of the Love equations. We focus in particular on one-dimensional quantum gases described by the Lieb-Liniger and the Gaudin-Yang models. For these models, the solutions of Love equations correspond to the normalized densities of pseudorapidities. We show that remarkably accurate results can be obtained using the Hermite-Chebyshev interpolation with a number of points as low as 17+2. In the case of the Lieb-Liniger model, we also propose a very accurate closed-form estimate of the dimensionless ground state energy valid for all values of the dimensionless interaction parameter incorporating known results for small and large couplings supplemented by the Hermite-Chebyshev interpolation for intermediate couplings. As an application of this result, we compute the dimensionless local two-particle correlation function and the black soliton solutions of the Lieb-Liniger model.

### Paper [74]
Title: Strain Controlled Hydrogen Interstitial Site Preference and Hydride Phase Suppression in Epitaxial Vanadium
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11276
Authors: Xin Xiao
Abstract: Understanding hydrogen behavior in transition metals is crucial for energy storage and sensing technologies. Here we combine density functional theory (DFT) calculations with in-situ electrical measurements to reveal how epitaxial strain governs hydrogen interstitial site selection and phase stability in vanadium. Our calculations demonstrate a robust thermodynamic preference for tetrahedral (T) over octahedral (O) sites, with the energy difference modulated by lattice anisotropy through strain-tunable H 1s-V 3d orbital hybridization. Experimentally, epitaxial V films on MgO(001) exhibit a critical thickness-dependent crossover: 50 nm films show characteristic first-order alpha-beta hydride transitions, while 10 nm films display continuous solid-solution behavior with complete transition suppression. This crossover is governed by the progressive relaxation of epitaxial strain with increasing film thickness: the 10 nm films retain strong coherent strain that flattens the free-energy landscape and suppresses hydride nucleation, whereas the 50 nm films approach the strain-relaxed bulk limit where long-range elastic interactions restore first-order phase behavior. Finite-size truncation of elastic fields and interfacial electronic reconstruction further reinforce this dimensional crossover. Our work establishes a unified framework connecting atomic-scale orbital physics to mesoscopic phase thermodynamics, providing design principles for strain-engineered metal-hydride systems.

### Paper [75]
Title: A Rule That's Made to be Broken? Reframing the Arrhenius Law for Ionic Transport
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11302
Authors: Vanessa K. Ward, Karen E. Johnston
Abstract: The Arrhenius law is ubiquitous in the physical sciences. It describes how an observable property scales with temperature, $T$, as $\exp(\pm E_A/k_BT)$ for a constant $E_A$, the 'activation energy'. Fitting to the Arrhenius law is a common procedure for both experimental and computational data. However, the physical significance of any 'activation energy' calculated is unclear. Here we focus on self-diffusion coefficients for potential solid electrolytes, and by identifying the individual 'diffusive steps' within the system, show how a physically significant activation energy can be extracted. There are also many examples of non-Arrhenius behaviour including `super-Arrhenius' curvature, where the effective activation energy appears to increase with decreasing temperature. We demonstrate how it is still possible to extract the underlying Arrhenius law, by quantifying self-correlations such as back-and-forth motion. The methodology described here is material-agnostic and can be utilised to analyse the molecular dynamics trajectories of both ordered and disordered materials.

### Paper [76]
Title: sdos: spectral densities of large sparse Hermitian and generalized eigenproblems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11310
Authors: David A. Drabold
Abstract: sdos is an open Python package that computes the density of states (DOS) and related spectral quantities of very large sparse Hermitian matrices A, and of generalized problems A psi = lambda B psi, from Chebyshev moments estimated with random vectors. The density is reconstructed by the kernel polynomial method (KPM, the default) or by maximum entropy (Maxent); the same interface gives band energies, Fermi levels, local and projected densities, harmonic thermodynamics, exact HOMO-LUMO gaps and eigenpairs in a spectral window. Interfaces cover electronic (empirical tight-binding, DFTB, SIESTA) and vibrational (matrix-free LAMMPS Hessians) problems. I checked most ingredients against dense diagonalization or an independent code; the exceptions are listed in Sec. V. On a workstation (4 threads, 19 GB) the DOS of an 884,736-atom amorphous silicon model (a 6^3 tiling of a 4096-atom cell; 3.5 million orbitals; the orthogonal empirical tight-binding model of Kwon et al.) takes 71 s (M=100, R=2, no exact moments; 383 s with the default exact moments), and a matrix-free 2,097,152-atom crystal 10 min in 1.8 GB. A 17^3 replica of a 216-atom amorphous cell (1,061,208 atoms) agrees with exact Bloch spectra: electronic DOS in 229 s, matrix-free ACE vibrational DOS in 2.2 h on one GPU. Shift-invert, incomplete-Cholesky setup, exact moments of high order and Maxent at high moment counts do not scale. The underlying methods are due to Silver, Roder, Kress, Voter, Drabold, Sankey and collaborators; the Introduction states what is added.

### Paper [77]
Title: Visualizing d-wave pairing symmetry at the atomic scale in a cuprate high-Tc superconductor by Josephson tunneling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11339
Authors: Zhen Liang, Tianheng Wei, Jinpeng Lu, Wei Ren, Takenori Fujii, Takao Watanabe, Ziqiang Wang, Jian Wang
Abstract: Superconducting pairing symmetry provides crucial insight into the microscopic mechanisms of Cooper pairing. However, probing the local manifestations of sign-changing superconducting order parameters remains experimentally challenging. Here, we investigate the superconducting condensate in optimally doped (OPT) Bi2Sr2Ca2Cu3O10+{\delta} (Bi-2223) using scanning Josephson tunneling microscopy (SJTM), equipped with an s-wave superconducting tip. Measurements at ultra-low temperatures reveal a pronounced modulation of the Josephson tunneling signal at different locations inside the crystallographic unit cell. A distinct Josephson tunneling signal is detected above O sites in the underlying CuO2 plane, whereas the signal nearly vanishes above the Cu-plaquette diagonals, including Cu sites and plaquette centers. This unique intra-unit-cell spatial pattern agrees with the theoretical prediction for the symmetry-dependent cancellation of Josephson tunneling between an s-wave superconducting tip and a d-wave superconducting sample. Our findings provide atomic-scale, real-space evidence for d-wave pairing symmetry in OPT high-Tc Bi-2223 and establish SJTM as a powerful approach for investigating the pairing symmetry of unconventional superconductors.

### Paper [78]
Title: Ergotropy as distance from equilibrium
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11343
Authors: Michele Campisi
Abstract: We show that the ergotropy (a.k.a available energy) of a thermally isolated system is the $1$-Wasserstein distance between two energy probability density functions. Specifically, between the energy profile of the system state, and the energy profile of its associated passive state. We prove this for both classical and quantum systems, and illustrate it with examples. Our result formalises the intuition that ergotropy is a quantifier of distance from equilibrium

### Paper [79]
Title: Crystal and magnetic structures of MnSi in zero magnetic field: Neutron diffraction and symmetry analysis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11353
Authors: Vladimir Pomjakushin, Igor Plokhikh, Douglas H. Fabini, Ekaterina Pomjakushina
Abstract: Combining high-resolution wide-angle neutron powder diffraction with magnetic-superspace symmetry analysis, we refine the crystal structure and present, to our knowledge, the first systematic refinement of cubic $P2_13$ MnSi's zero-field magnetic structure across multiple magnetic satellite families. We compare single-arm rhombohedral 3+1D and cubic four-arm 3+4D magnetic superspace-group (MSSG) descriptions with propagation vectors along $\langle111\rangle$. Simultaneous extinction of the two satellite groups around $\{100\}$ dictates a transverse circular-helix model. This suppression and refinements of 18 separately varied satellite-group intensities support this ideal limit with only one adjustable magnetic amplitude. In the ideal single-arm model with MSSG $R3.1'(0,0,g)ts$, the moment has constant magnitude 0.535(3) $\mu_B$ per Mn. An explicit four-arm 3+4D cubic superspace construction presented here combines four interpenetrating helices and, for the illustrated phase choice, has moments ranging from zero to 0.874(5) $\mu_B$ per Mn, with the same root-mean-square moment as the 3+1D structure. Their identical powder profiles prevent the present measurements from distinguishing them. The average crystal structure remains cubic within experimental resolution, providing structural motivation for the cubic magnetic model. The mean lattice parameter, determined with formal relative precision $\sigma_a/a \lesssim 10^{-5}$, exhibits nonmonotonic temperature dependence below the N\'eel temperature.

### Paper [80]
Title: Industrial RPCVD growth of high-purity 28Si epilayers with 29Si content below 30 ppm for high-fidelity electron spin qubits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11405
Authors: Zhenzhen Kong, Weizhu Liao, Ning Chu, Yiwen Zhang, Ying Zhang, Jixuan Li, Changyang Xu, Yongliang Hao, Yi Zhou, Yanpeng Song, Xiaomeng Liu, Hailing Wang, Xiangsheng Wang, Jinbiao Liu, Chao Zhao, Hao Meng, Gang Cao, Guo-Ping Guo, Hai-Ou Li, Guilei Wang
Abstract: Silicon quantum-dot spin qubits are among the most promising routes to large-scale quantum computation, but the residual 29Si nuclear spins in natural silicon impose a hyperfine dephasing limit that must be overcome by isotopic purification. Here, we report the epitaxial growth of high-purity 28Si on float-zone silicon using an industrial reduced-pressure chemical vapor deposition (RPCVD) system. Secondary-ion mass spectrometry (SIMS) confirms an 28Si isotopic purity of 99.997%, with the 29Si concentration below 30 ppm. Through a systematic vacuum annealing matrix (850-1150 C), combined with atomic force microscopy and SIMS, we investigate the defect-mediated decomposition of the native oxide and the injection of non-equilibrium vacancies. We identify a thermal-budget window below 950 C within which the thin-layer structure of 28Si remains intact. At 1.5 K, magnetotransport measurements reveal a peak Hall mobility of 12,858 cm^2 V^-1 s^-1 and a quantum scattering lifetime of 0.659 ps, corresponding to a single-particle level broadening of 499 micro-eV. A double quantum dot fabricated on this platform exhibits a T2* of 0.62 us, a Hahn-echo coherence time of 28.8 us, and single-qubit gate fidelities of up to 99.9% measured by randomized benchmarking. Our results establish industrial RPCVD as a promising route toward high-coherence, high-fidelity silicon spin qubits.

### Paper [81]
Title: Orbital texture of valence band and interband transitions of the type-II multiferroic van der Waals NiI$_2$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11425
Authors: A. De Vita, O. E. Giehl, T. Mukherjee, M. Wolf, L. Rettig, R. Ernstorfer, T. Pincelli, L. T. Lloyd
Abstract: NiI$_2$ is a van der Waals semiconductor characterized by complex electronic and magnetic phases, holding promise towards functional applications. For this purpose, a detailed understanding of its band structure and its excitations is essential to elucidate the electronic and transport properties. We present a comprehensive description of the band structure and of the lowest valence-to-conduction band transition in bulk NiI$_2$, by means of time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy supported by first-principles calculations. We describe the orbital character of Ni and I manifolds, and identify the main momentum-resolved features of the valence band and the shallow minima of the conduction band. With this study, we provide a foundational understanding of the electronic structure of NiI$_2$ and its single-particle interband electronic excitation, a benchmark for theoretical models, and a framework for future investigations on this material.

### Paper [82]
Title: Momentum-dependent phonon effects on spin-fluctuation $d$-wave pairing in bulk La$_6$Ni$_5$O$_{12}$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11453
Authors: Dexi Shao, Zhiming Pan, Ming Zhang, Yupeng Li, Chen Lu
Abstract: We study the effect of electron-phonon coupling on spin-fluctuation-mediated pairing in bulk La$_6$Ni$_5$O$_{12}$. A five-layer Hubbard model treated within the random-phase approximation yields a leading $d_{x^2-y^2}$ pairing state. Two phenomenological couplings motivated by oxygen breathing modes favor $s$-wave pairing when considered alone, while their attractive scattering between opposite-sign regions of the spin-fluctuation-induced gap suppresses $d$-wave pairing. Electronic charge screening strongly reduces this suppression. Comparing the $s$- and $d$-wave pairing strengths with and without screening shows that screening shifts their boundary in favor of $d$-wave pairing. Momentum-resolved projections locate the reduction of the unfavorable scattering processes. At the representative electronic interaction, $d$-wave pairing remains dominant.

### Paper [83]
Title: Giant gas permeance through a nanoporous crystalline 2D polymer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11518
Authors: Jonathan D'Emidio, Daniel J. Murray, William B. Thompson, Jarvis C. Humlick, K. C. Schwab, Adrian Del Maestro, Benjamin T. King
Abstract: Chemical separation processes represent one of the largest contributors to industrial energy consumption, underscoring the need for the most efficient separation strategies. Many separation processes rely on selective transport through porous media. In this context, two-dimensional (2D) materials offer a promising route to atomically precise porosity beyond the reach of traditional top-down fabrication, with the potential to enhance separation performance by increasing flow. Here we report measurements of gas transport through a one-molecule-thick layer of suspended, nanoporous poly(carboxyfantrip), a 2D polymer that has identical, nanometer-sized pores that cover nearly a quarter of the surface. This combination of ultimate thinness with high areal pore density gives normalized gas flows (permeances) that approach the effusion limit, three to four orders of magnitude above gas-sieving graphene membranes. We find that gases spanning nearly two orders of magnitude in molecular weight (H$_2$, He, N$_2$, CO$_2$, and SF$_6$) obey Graham's law and approach the ideal limit of non-interacting ballistic transport, despite strong, species-dependent gas-pore interactions. Simulations reproduce the measured permeances within experimental uncertainty and provide an atomistic picture of the transport process. This work demonstrates the potential of synthetic crystalline 2D polymers for chemical separation, water filtration, and quantum sensing.

### Paper [84]
Title: Overcoming speed-accuracy trade-offs with medium-sized interatomic potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11544
Authors: Flynn Walsh, Nicolas Bertin, Robert E. Rudd, Aur\'elien Perron
Abstract: Current machine-learned interatomic potentials offer a choice between small, fast models and large, expressive networks. This study demonstrates how an intermediate potential can approach the limit of transferable accuracy at the cost of the cheapest existing formalisms. Specifically, we model He-bearing W-Ta-Hf-Cr-V-Ti alloys with an atomic cluster expansion that learns the radial components of each polynomial as independent linear transforms and embeds numerous densities with learned exponents. While (near-)training loss appears largely a function of model size, the error on a distinct validation set is close to that of leading potentials with ten times more parameters. Taking advantage of the new structure, we develop optimized implementations that run more than an order of magnitude faster than the larger models and many times faster than smaller models with equivalent polynomials. The potential is trained on systematically enumerated small structures but generalizes to point and planar defects and can be extended to even more complex chemistries. As a demonstration, we simulate radiation-damage cascades in billion-atom systems for hundreds of thousands of timesteps.

### Paper [85]
Title: Ballistic transport through 3D flat bands in rhombohedral graphene in an in-plane magnetic field
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11603
Authors: Eran Sela, Rafi Bistritzer, Yuval Oreg
Abstract: We explore how an in-plane magnetic field reshapes the transmitting channels of a finite slab of rhombohedral multilayer graphene and redistributes the current across its thickness. The two-terminal conductance counts these channels, while the voltage at each surface reflects the relative spectral weights of right- and left-moving states on the contacted layer. In sufficiently thick slabs or at sufficiently strong fields, the nearly flat bulk zeroth Landau level gives rise to a three-dimensional Hall effect with a plateau in the four-terminal Hall resistance.

### Paper [86]
Title: Coherent Control of High-Order Lightwave Currents in Gate-Tunable Bilayer Graphene
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11616
Authors: Samuel Haeuser, Martin Mootz, Dasol Kim, Avinash Khatri, Joong-Mok Park, Randall K. Chan, Richard H. J. Kim, Yongxing Yao, Ilias E. Perakis, Hou-Tong Chen, Thomas Koschny, Long Ju, Jigang Wang
Abstract: The sub-cycle acceleration of charge carriers in an electronic circuit by tailored terahertz (THz) electric-field waveforms, rather than conventional pulsed bias voltages, opens a transformative frontier for lightwave electronics operating far beyond the scaling limits of gigahertz electronic technologies. Yet gate-tunable coherent control of multi-order and non-perturbative currents has remained elusive. Here, we demonstrate lightwave control over the amplitude, phase flips, and zero-crossings of THz field-driven high-order currents by utilizing phase-locked pairs of single-cycle THz fields in a dual-gated bilayer graphene (BLG) device. By leveraging the in-situ tuning of both bandgap and charge doping, we reveal extreme THz photocurrent rectification and sub-THz-cycle current generation that are governed by a strong field-driven crossover from interband multi-photon coherence to intraband lightwave acceleration, whose competition provides an exceptional sensitivity to the THz-pulse pair drive. Our interferometric nonlinear THz photocurrent spectroscopy of sub-cycle electronic excitations accesses and controls non-perturbative, multi-quantum photocurrents, which manifest as an extreme-order THz frequency comb. Quantum-kinetic simulations map these lightwave-controlled electron trajectories, revealing that sub-THz-cycle ballistic transport underpins gate- and THz-field-driven functionality enabled by electrostatic doping and bandgap tuning within the atomically thin device architecture. The demonstrated on-demand coherent control of nonperturbative photocurrent transport regimes can be extended to a broad range of atomically thin device architectures, opening new avenues for gate- and field-programmable logic, memory, and quantum simulators in which electronic pathways are steered on sub-cycle, petahertz timescales and atomic length scales.

### Paper [87]
Title: Giant magnetoresistance rectification by N\'eel vector dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11654
Authors: Zhiyi Che, Yifang Hao, Aakanksha Sud, Murat Cubukcu, Charlie W. F. Freeman, Chenghao Yang, Daniel Prestwood, Troy Dion, Shunsuke Fukami, Shigemi Mizukam, Hidekazu Kurebayashi
Abstract: We observe room-temperature giant magnetoresistance (GMR) rectification driven by N\'eel vector dynamics in synthetic antiferromagnets. During spin-torque ferromagnetic resonance, the out-of-phase (optical) mode makes the relative angle between the two sub-moments oscillate, producing a rectified voltage due to GMR. This component is isolated from anisotropic and spin Hall magnetoresistance contributions by its angular symmetry, with its voltages scaling linearly with microwave power. We extract a GMR coefficient of 0.18-0.20 % from the rectification, which agrees with independently measured dc magnetoresistance. Reversing the N\'eel vector with a dc current changes the sign of the GMR rectification voltage in a non-volatile and controlled manner.

### Paper [88]
Title: Hydrogen Thermodiffusion in Tungsten: Trapped Hydrogen Distorts Heat of Transport Estimation from Total Concentration Profiles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11702
Authors: Ville Jantunen, Daniel R. Mason, Sanjeet Kaur
Abstract: We investigate hydrogen diffusion in tungsten under thermal gradients in the presence of trapping sites using multiscale simulations. Using molecular dynamics simulations, we find that the heat of transport, which quantifies the coupling between temperature gradients and hydrogen transport, for hydrogen in tungsten is positive (thermophobic) at lower temperatures but changes sign near $1000$ K. We investigate the origin of the heat of transport and find that harmonic transition state theory with elastic thermal-expansion corrections is insufficient to explain the observed behaviour, consistent with either a breakdown of local thermodynamic equilibrium or non-negligible anharmonic contributions.
  We further extend previous studies of hydrogen thermodiffusion by including the effect of trapping sites in both simulations and analytical models. We show that when trapped hydrogen accounts for a significant fraction of the total hydrogen concentration, the heat of transport inferred from measurements of the total concentration is dominated by the temperature dependence of the trapped hydrogen population and no longer reflects the gradient in the mobile hydrogen concentration. Consequently, experimentally measured heats of transport cannot in general be used directly in continuum-scale models that explicitly account for trapping and detrapping. In such models, the heat of transport should be based on mobile hydrogen concentrations only to avoid incorporating trapping effects twice.

### Paper [89]
Title: Strain-dependent Dynamics of Interactions of Iodine-vacancy pairs in CsPbI3: A DFT and Machine-Learning Molecular Dynamics Study
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11765
Authors: Donggeun Kang, Seongju Lee, Yonggoon Jeon
Abstract: Iodine-vacancy migration and association are important factors in the structural stability of halide perovskites. Although neighboring vacancies have been suggested to form energetically favorable pairs, their stability and reconfiguration under finite-temperature lattice deformation remain unclear. Here, we combine machine-learning molecular dynamics (MLMD) and density functional theory (DFT) to investigate neighboring iodine vacancies in tilted CsPbI3 under isotropic and biaxial strain. The MLMD trajectories reveal a nonmonotonic dependence of short-time hopping fractions on strain, with strong isotropic compression favoring point-like separation and tension promoting pair-preserving relay hopping. Notably, biaxial compression produces distinct orientation-dependent responses: vacancy pairs initially aligned with the compressed plane preferentially undergo separation, whereas those perpendicular to the plane predominantly exhibit relay hopping. Complementary static calculations using DFT and the SevenNet-0 potential yield repulsive interactions for all configurations examined. These findings highlight the importance of finite-temperature dynamics and strain-dependent local geometry in understanding vacancy-pair stability and reconfiguration in halide perovskites.

### Paper [90]
Title: Frustrated Magnetism from Dimerized $S = 1/2$ Co$^{2+}$ in Rose-Phase Na$_2$CoP$_2$O$_7$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11772
Authors: Shukun Li, Leon Ma, Xianghan Xu
Abstract: We report the growth of high-quality single crystals and the magnetic, thermodynamic, and dielectric properties of rose-phase Na$_2$CoP$_2$O$_7$, a low-symmetry magnet in which corner-sharing Co$^{2+}$ dimers form an approximately triangular network. Magnetic measurements reveal dominant antiferromagnetic interactions and pronounced anisotropy among effective $S=1/2$ Co$^{2+}$ moments. Broad low-temperature maxima in the magnetic susceptibility and heat capacity suggest the development of short-range magnetic correlations below approximately 4 K. The large separation between the absolute Curie--Weiss temperatures and the characteristic correlation temperature indicates strong magnetic frustration arising from competing interactions within the coupled-dimer network. Applied magnetic fields suppress these anomalies and reduce the recovered magnetic entropy, suggesting further enhancement of frustration under field. A systematic magnetodielectric response closely follows the field dependence of the magnetization, indicating coupling between magnetic and dielectric degrees of freedom. The low lattice symmetry enables multiple inequivalent exchange pathways and anisotropic interactions while relaxing symmetry constraints on coupling among spin, lattice, and dielectric degrees of freedom. These results establish rose-phase Na$_2$CoP$_2$O$_7$ as a platform for exploring frustrated quantum magnetism and coupled functionalities in effective $S=1/2$ magnets.

### Paper [91]
Title: Supercurrent-controlled magnon transport via suppression of the superconducting gap
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11781
Authors: Tetsuma Mandokoro, Yoichi Shiota, Hisakazu Matsuki, Ryusuke Hisatomi, Shutaro Karube, Akito Daido, Youichi Yanase, Teruo Ono
Abstract: Understanding interactions between different low-energy excitations is a central theme in condensed-matter physics. Magnons and superconducting condensates represent two distinct macroscopic quantum states, yet their mutual interaction remains largely unexplored. Here, we demonstrate supercurrent-controlled magnon transport in a ferromagnet/superconductor heterostructure. We observe that the magnon transport signal decreases as the supercurrent increases. This suppression originates from a supercurrent-induced reduction of the superconducting gap, which enhances magnon dissipation into the superconductor via Bogoliubov quasiparticles. Our results provide direct experimental evidence of a supercurrent-controlled interaction between magnons and quasiparticles in the superconductor. The approach used in this study relies on supercurrents, enabling magnon modulation without Joule heating. These findings establish a new route to control magnons via superconductivity and pave the way for energy-efficient magnonic devices.

### Paper [92]
Title: More particles or the pair? An exact test of how to improve mode-coupling theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11806
Authors: Hu Cang
Abstract: How a dense liquid arrests into a glass remains a central problem in physics. Mode-coupling theory (MCT), the leading microscopic theory of the glass transition, fails to reproduce mean-field caging. Two routes are proposed to repair it: tracking more-particle correlations, or enforcing repulsion within each colliding pair. Testing both on an exact benchmark liquid, we show that MCT lets touching spheres pass through each other at all orders: adding particles dilutes memory and worsens the transition, whereas restoring pair impenetrability recovers mean-field arrest and the cage size.

### Paper [93]
Title: The Photonic Bath at Infinity: Foundations, Derivations, and Consistency Checks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11816
Authors: Jian-Sheng Wang
Abstract: The photonic bath at infinity represents the unbounded electromagnetic environment as an effective reservoir in the nonequilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism. It accounts for outgoing radiation and incoming electromagnetic fluctuations, allowing radiative transport to be described consistently. We clarify how environmental fluctuations are incorporated without double-counting the outgoing radiation response already contained in the free retarded Green's function. We develop four complementary approaches to deriving the bath self-energy: infinitesimal environmental absorption, semi-infinite leads, inversion of the free Green's function, and sesquilinear forms with outgoing radiation boundary conditions. In particular, we present an explicit three-site matrix inversion for a planar geometry and introduce a sesquilinear form that relates the bath self-energy to the Silver--M\"uller radiation condition. We establish the connections among these approaches and obtain explicit bath self-energies for one-dimensional, planar, and spherical geometries. The resulting bath self-energies reproduce the Planck spectrum, the Stefan--Boltzmann law, and radiation pressure, and provide the radiative contribution required for energy conservation in photon NEGF.

### Paper [94]
Title: How Path Integral Diffusion Decides a Finite Spin Glass
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11826
Authors: Michael Chertkov
Abstract: When an AI diffusion-type generator samples a frustrated spin glass, which macroscopic alternatives does it decide first, and when? We study this for bridge-diffusion models of the Path Integral Diffusion (PID) type started from a point source. We show that every isotropic PID is a time-changed Gaussian stochastic localization, and conversely every signal-to-noise schedule is realized by a PID protocol. What a continuous-time generator decides therefore does not depend on the protocol, which only sets when and at what cost. We describe the generator's state by its partial commitments: the probabilities it assigns, at each moment, to nested classes of configurations fixed by the target. The partial commitments are martingales that move only along class contrasts. Their ensemble average gives the information about each level at an exact rate set by squared magnetization contrasts. Each split is decided, approximately, like a binary Gaussian channel at signal-to-noise ratio 1.04/\lambda_P, with \lambda_P the leading eigenvalue of the parent's correlation matrix. The theory is applied to Bethe-lattice and Sherrington--Kirkpatrick (SK) glasses with up to 64 spins. It is observed generically that a trajectory first shifts its weight to one of the two mirror halves of the zero-field measure and then to the glassy classes inside it. These classes appear near the glass transition and keep their identity on cooling. Nested glassy levels have similar \lambda_P and are decided together, so a trajectory commits in one or two distinct events---the mirror choice and a compound glassy decision---which separate further with coupling and size, as the spectra predict. For a generator that takes finitely many steps, the protocol matters through the late, local stage rather than the macroscopic decisions.

### Paper [95]
Title: Computing Classical Partition Functions via Cycle-Space Sampling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11850
Authors: Yoav Feigenbaum, Lev Barash, Itay Hen
Abstract: We present a method for computing thermodynamic properties of classical spin systems by reformulating the partition function as a sum over even subgraphs of the interaction graph. Starting from the high-temperature expansion, we show that the expansion coefficients are determined by the weight distribution of the null space of the binary incidence matrix over $\mathbb{F}_2$, and that this statement holds verbatim for multi-spin interactions once the graph is replaced by a hypergraph. We introduce a Monte Carlo scheme based on the $1/t$ Wang-Landau algorithm that performs a random walk directly in this null space. Because the weight distribution is temperature independent, a single estimate yields the zero-field partition function, and the thermodynamic quantities obtained from its temperature derivatives, at arbitrary temperature. We validate the approach against exact finite-size results on the two-dimensional triangular-lattice Ising model, apply it to the three- and four-dimensional hypercubic Ising models where no exact solution is available, and to the two- and three-dimensional Baxter-Wu models, whose three-spin interactions place them outside the reach of Pfaffian methods. For the two-body models we compare against conventional Wang-Landau sampling in energy space; for the Baxter-Wu models we validate against exact enumeration at the smallest size in each dimension.

### Paper [96]
Title: Topology to Elasticity Mapping in Thermal Glass
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11939
Authors: Avinash Kumar Jha
Abstract: Building on our previous work on thermal nonaffine metrics and thermal strain fields, we examine how local steric geometry controls thermally driven rearrangements in an amorphous glass-forming solid. As a structural descriptor, we employ the topological steric "theta-mismatch" order parameter, which quantifies local deviations of bond angles from the steric reference geometry and is used to distinguish low- and high-mismatch particle populations. We then compare their thermal nonaffine response, thermal deviatoric strain, and thermal volumetric strain over a broad temperature range at several state points. High-Delta-theta sites preferentially exhibit larger thermal nonaffine motion and stronger thermal deviatoric strain, particularly in the deeply jammed regime, indicating that geometrically frustrated regions are preferentially associated with localized mechanical distortions. The thermal volumetric response exhibits a distinct mismatch-dependent crossover: low-Delta-theta sites predominantly contribute to the small-strain response, whereas high-Delta-theta sites contribute more strongly to the large-strain tail. The contrast between low- and high-mismatch populations is most pronounced in the deeply jammed regime, remains identifiable through the intermediate viscoelastic regime, and progressively weakens upon heating toward the liquid state. These results establish a direct connection between local steric frustration and thermally activated mechanical response, showing that thermal nonaffine metrics and thermal strain fields provide a physically transparent bridge between local structure and thermally driven rearrangements in amorphous glasses.

### Paper [97]
Title: Frank Elastic Anisotropy Induced by Anisotropic Attractive Interactions in Nematic Liquid Crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11954
Authors: Akihiko Matsuyama
Abstract: We present a mean-field theory to calculate the Frank elastic constants of a nematic liquid crystal with an anisotropic Maier--Saupe (MS) attractive interaction restricted to a cylindrical interaction volume of height $L_z$ and diameter $L_c$. The shape of the interaction volume is characterized by the aspect ratio $\alpha_z=L_z/L_c$, which controls the spatial anisotropy of the attractive interaction. We derive expressions for the splay $(K_{11})$, twist $(K_{22})$, and bend $(K_{33})$ elastic constants and calculate them numerically as functions of concentration and temperature. At the special aspect ratio $\alpha_z=\sqrt{3}/2$, the transverse and axial second moments are equal, yielding the one-constant relation $K_{11}=K_{22}=K_{33}$ over the full ranges of concentration and temperature considered. Away from this point, oblate and prolate interaction volumes produce distinct elastic anisotropies. Changes in concentration or temperature can also reverse the hierarchy of the three elastic constants.

### Paper [98]
Title: Magnetotropic signatures of Fulde-Ferrell Pairing in a Pauli-limited superconductor
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12032
Authors: Gulnaz Rakhmanova, Mikhail Lemeshko, Kimberly A. Modic, Ragheed Alhyder
Abstract: Resonant torsion magnetometry measures the angular curvature of the free energy and is sensitive to magnetic anisotropy. We calculate its response for a clean Pauli-limited superconductor with single-plane-wave Fulde-Ferrell pairing. Field rotation drives a first-order transition from the uniform condensate into the finite-momentum phase and a continuous transition into the normal state. The first-order line produces a sharp softening whose integrated weight is fixed by the magnetization jump, so the dip alone does not identify finite momentum. The distinguishing response is a broad softening inside the Fulde-Ferrell phase, caused by relaxation of the gap and pairing momentum, followed by a finite step at the upper critical field.

### Paper [99]
Title: Connate Topological Superconductivity and Vortex-Position-Controlled Majorana Modes in the Intergrowth Superconductor Th$_2$Mo$_2$Ir$_2$Si$_4$C
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12080
Authors: Jiayang Li, Qing Wang, Xun-Jiang Luo, Mingliang Tian, Ning Hao
Abstract: The recently synthesized intergrowth compound Th$_2$Mo$_2$Ir$_2$Si$_4$C is an experimentally established bulk superconductor whose topological electronic structure remains unexplored. Here, we identify it as a candidate for connate topological superconductivity and reveal two microscopic routes to vortex Majorana zero modes. First-principles and tight-binding-model calculations uncover Mo--Th band inversion and helical surface states enabled by interorbital spin--orbit coupling between Mo--d$_{xz/yz}$ and Th--d$_{x^2-y^2}$ orbitals. Vortex Bogoliubov--de Gennes calculations further establish a relatively robust surface-state-associated Kitaev phase and a weakly gapped, Th-orbital-derived Kitaev phase. For the latter, shifting the vortex core closes and reopens the vortex-line gap and reverses its one-dimensional topological invariant, consistent with an effective Kitaev-chain mass inversion. Finite-length-vortex calculations reveal distinct spatial and spin-resolved signatures of their Majorana end modes. The orbital Kitaev phase can also exist without helical surface states in a modified model. Although the small orbital-derived gap limits quantitative predictions of experimental observability in this compound, the uncovered mechanism suggests a route to Majorana vortices in multiorbital superconductors beyond the conventional surface-state scenario.

### Paper [100]
Title: Where reconfiguration makes a structure stronger
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12081
Authors: Iman Mohagheghian
Abstract: Mechanical metamaterials can reconfigure on command and learn, so adaptation is a central promise of the field. Can reconfiguration make a metamaterial stronger? The answer depends on the comparison. Against the best fixed design of the same mass, the advantage of reconfiguring is often exactly zero. Reconfiguring helps only if the self-stress the actuators add lowers every member at its limit simultaneously. It vanishes where a member at its limit is one no self-stress can change, which the geometry fixes, or where those members are coupled so that lowering one raises another, which depends on the geometry and the loads together. Where neither block applies the gain can be large, as in a twelve-spoke hub with its worst member utilisation reduced by 29.1%. Whether reconfiguration is worthwhile is decided by how the self-stress of a structure meets the members setting its capacity under load, and can be tested in advance.

### Paper [101]
Title: Topology, geometry and fabrication of volumetric plain weaves
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12091
Authors: Samuel Poincloux, Lauren Carelli Dreier, Tian Chen
Abstract: Plain weave is one of the simplest woven structures, formed by two sets of yarns that cross at right angles in an alternating over-under pattern. While such woven fabrics are usually confined to a 2D surface, here we propose a practical approach to fabricate volumetric plain weaves, entanglements featuring three mutually orthogonal families of yarns threaded through a cubic lattice. We develop an analytic framework that converts the crossing pattern topology into continuous yarn centreline geometries, which we then leverage to fabricate volumetric weave specimens. Each crossing is encoded by a matrix that defines the offsets of the three yarns at a vertex. These are formulated to preserve the plain weave topology in every plane, where each pair of yarn families interlaces as in a planar plain weave. Eight configurations in four opposite pairs exist, yet all are related by rotations and translations, so that the fully alternating volumetric plain weave has a single topology. We adopt sinusoidal yarn trajectories and show that the crossing matrix defines their phases. We select two solutions: in-phase planar zigzags and out-of-phase circular helices. Both are members of a family of elliptical helices that also admits a free choice of handedness for each yarn. We then derive the conditions under which yarns intersect between vertices. In-phase yarns never intersect, whereas helical yarns of equal handedness intersect at a critical offset of $\sqrt{2}\lambda/16$, with $\lambda$ the wavelength of the cubic lattice. No assignment of handedness avoids these intersections. Finally, we fabricate volumetric plain weaves by 3D printing and manual assembly from cotton cord. The framework provides a direct basis for generating weave geometries for simulation and fabrication.

### Paper [102]
Title: Spatial effects of Kuramoto modeling on the power-grid frequencies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12132
Authors: Krist\'of Benedek, Michelle T. Cirunay, Sangita Dutta, Attila Balogh, B\'alint Hartmann, G\'eza \'Odor
Abstract: Spatial coupling and heterogeneity can generate complex power-grid frequency dynamics. We investigate the contributions of spatial structure, intrinsic nonlinear dynamics, and external driving using the Kuramoto model (KM), with and without uncorrelated noise, and compare its predictions with real-grid measurements.
  We analyse one month of 100 ms frequency measurements from 16 Hungarian transmission-grid sites and simulate the KM on a 387-node transmission network and two-dimensional lattices. The measured power spectra exhibit two scaling regimes, with exponents of approximately 1.63 and 2.23. One-second frequency increments decorrelate rapidly, while correlations of their local inverse variance decay slowly, initially as a power law, $\tau^{-0.26(2)}$, up to 15 minutes. A superstatistical Gaussian mixture based on measured local variances captures much of the non-Gaussian increment distribution, but not the rare extreme events.
  We also estimate the non-stationary long-time frequency autocorrelation exponent. KM simulations on low-dimensional networks exhibit algebraically decaying autocorrelations and power spectra, consistent with the measurements and the Wiener-Khinchin relation in the steady state. On the heterogeneous transmission network, partial synchronization and frequency locking produce persistent spatial patterns and multi-peaked frequency distributions. However, neither deterministic KM dynamics nor independent Gaussian driving reproduces the measured spectra and increment statistics simultaneously. Moreover, empirical frequency fluctuations are largely shared across substations, whereas simulated fluctuations mainly reflect relative node motion. These findings suggest that spatially coupled nonlinear dynamics contribute to observed frequency fluctuations alongside external driving, including 15-minute power dispatches.

### Paper [103]
Title: Scaling up spin complexity in ultracold assembled molecules: association and quantum chemistry of Cr-bearing diatomics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12159
Authors: Beatrice Restivo, Stefano Finelli, Andrea Simoni, Andreas Trenkwalder, Matteo Zaccanti, Alessio Ciamei
Abstract: The availability of ever more atomic elements in the ultracold regime potentially enables the extension of molecule assembly well beyond bi-alkali systems to targeted chemical species for novel quantum science and technologies. The concurrent need for single-quantum-state control and molecular constants from \emph{ab initio} calculations, especially for previously unexplored species, creates a trade-off between complexity and feasibility. Here, we address this challenge and exploit inter-isotope Feshbach spectroscopy as a tool to study three chromium-bearing diatomics ($^6$Li$^X$Cr, $^{50,52}$Cr$_2$, and $^{53}$Cr$_2$) at different levels of complexity. The extracted molecular properties, including the number of vibrational levels and the dispersion coefficients, allow us to assess quantum chemistry methods. Moreover, our understanding of atomic scattering allows us to assemble LiCr and Cr$_2$ molecules and clarify the observed association pathways with quantum state selectivity. Our results suggest unique prospects for the Li-Cr and Cr-Cr systems, and, more importantly, the experimental viability of a wider class of assembled high-spin molecules for many-body physics and precision measurements.

### Paper [104]
Title: Misorientations in dislocation cell structures: Modelling and statistical analysis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12165
Authors: Lei Zeng, Yufan Zhang, Michael Zaiser
Abstract: Dislocation pattern formation is accompanied by the emergence of lattice rotations and concomitant misorientations across dislocation walls (cell boundaries). Recent advances in mesoscopic modelling of dislocation patterns enable for the first time a systematic simulation study of this phenomenon.
