We present simulation results for lattice rotation and misorientation patterns in crystals with face-centered cubic and rocksalt lattice structure. Statistical analysis of the orientation changes across cell walls reveals a transition associated with a symmetry-breaking bifurcation in slip system activity: Below the bifurcation, statistical tests indicate the presence of a single population of misoriented walls whereas, above the bifurcation, two sub-populations of 'incidental' and 'geometrically necessary' dislocation walls are present. Our data analytic approach demonstrates that this distinction does not require prior image segmentation and emphasizes the statistical robustness of the distinction between two types of dislocation walls.

### Paper [105]
Title: Quantum-geometry-controlled pseudogap and non-Fermi-liquid behavior
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12168
Authors: Yu Miura, Youichi Yanase
Abstract: The quantum metric plays an essential role in flat-band superconductivity, governing the superfluid weight and coherence length. While previous studies have primarily focused on mean-field superconducting properties, the role of quantum geometry in fluctuation phenomena remains largely unexplored, despite the importance of fluctuations in flat-band systems. To address this issue, we employ a multiorbital T-matrix approximation that retains the orbital structure of the Bloch wave functions. By analyzing models in which the quantum metric can be tuned independently of the band dispersion, we show that an enhanced quantum metric suppresses the fluctuation-induced pseudogap in both flat-band and dispersive-band systems. In dispersive-band systems, this suppression is accompanied by a crossover from non-Fermi-liquid to Fermi-liquid behavior. This spectral evolution suggests a quantum-metric-driven crossover from a BEC-like strong-coupling regime to a BCS-like weak-coupling regime. More broadly, our results establish the quantum metric as a control parameter of interaction-driven fluctuation phenomena beyond mean-field theory.

### Paper [106]
Title: Phonon emission due to dc-driven electron heating in a two-dimensional electron gas containing artificial scatterers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12185
Authors: Frederik Bartels, Mihai Cerchez, Jan Lange, Dominique Mailly, Klaus Pierz, Hans W. Schumacher, Thomas Heinzel
Abstract: We report the observation of phonon-induced resistance oscillations (PIROs) up to order $\varepsilon_\mathrm{{ph}} = 8$ in a two-dimensional electron gas containing artificial scatterers, activated by electron heating due to an applied dc current which enables phonon emission. Under equilibrium conditions, only two weak PIRO maxima are detectable. The dc current simultaneously generates Hall field induced resistance oscillations (HIROs). A precise analysis of their amplitude, which is modulated by the double resonance with PIROs, dramatically enhances the visibility of the latter compared to the equilibrium case. We identify transverse acoustic phonons with a sound velocity of $s = 3.4$\,km/s as the relevant mode and analyze the oscillatory amplitude modulation in the subsonic regime ($\varepsilon_{\mathrm{dc}} < \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ph}}$) within the theoretical framework of Dmitriev \textit{et al.} At the sound barrier ($\varepsilon_{\mathrm{dc}} = \varepsilon_{\mathrm{ph}}$), where the electron drift velocity reaches the speed of sound, we observe a phase shift in the oscillatory electron-phonon interaction that independently confirms recent experimental findings.

### Paper [107]
Title: Lattice Commensurability and Majorana sectors of Pfaffian--Anti-Pfaffian interfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12195
Authors: Feng Liu, Xue-Yang Song
Abstract: We study Pfaffian and anti-Pfaffian non-Abelian fractional quantum anomalous Hall states in half-filled moir\'{e} lattice systems. Owing to crystal-momentum conservation modulo a reciprocal lattice vector, these lattice systems admit coupled-wire constructions of the two phases without requiring spatially varying magnetic-field-induced momentum shifts. For the resulting Pfaffian--anti-Pfaffian interface, a Umklapp process can gap the counter-propagating charge sector, while the remaining four co-propagating Majorana modes naturally group into two sectors organized by translation symmetries. Our results show that lattice commensurability qualitatively changes the coupled-wire accessibility of non-Abelian states while providing a microscopic momentum structure that directly constrains their edge and interface dynamics.

### Paper [108]
Title: Plasmon excitations in monolayer stanene
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12212
Authors: Jia-Hui Qin, Yunhua Wang
Abstract: Monolayer stanene exhibits strong intrinsic spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and notably distinct low-energy band features involving both $\Gamma$ and K valleys together with strong electron-hole asymmetry among group-IV monolayers. Its plasmonic properties and external-field tunability remain largely unclarified. Here, we systematically investigate its high-energy plasmon structures and low-energy collective excitation modulated by temperature, electrostatic doping and perpendicular electric field. Calculations use a $sp^3$ tight-binding model with SOC and sublattice staggered potentials, combined with the finite-temperature multiband Lindhard function under the random-phase approximation. Besides the $\pi$ and $\pi+\sigma$ plasmon known in graphene/silicene, stanene supports two new high-energy plasmon branches. For low-energy plasmons, thermal excitation extends the accessible energy-momentum range while preserving high quality factors and collective coherence. Electrostatic doping yields distinct plasmon behaviors: moderate doping gives K-valley dominated intraband $\sqrt{q}$ Dirac plasmons; heavy electron doping retains $\sqrt{q}$ dispersion via Drude contributions from $\Gamma$ and K valleys; heavy hole doping triggers interband transitions near the $\Gamma$ point, producing new $\sigma$-$\sigma$ and $\sigma$-$\sigma^*$ interband plasmon modes and complex multi-branch spectra. Furthermore, the perpendicular electric field enables continuous tuning of the $\sqrt{q}$ plasmon via band reconstruction below 0.3 V/{\AA} and induces extra plasmon modes near band-gap closure. These tunable low-energy plasmons span terahertz-to-mid-infrared frequencies, promising for nanophotonic applications.

### Paper [109]
Title: Impact of multi-dot configurations on electron temperature and double quantum dot latching in gate-controlled qubit devices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12217
Authors: Jakob Walsh, Miriam Schweinb\"ock, Jhih-Sian Tu, Yuji Yamamoto, Felix Reichmann, Lars R. Schreiber, Dominique Bougeard
Abstract: Many currently envisaged scalable architectures for Si/SiGe spin qubit devices utilize gate-controlled multi-dot configurations characterized by their peculiar Coulomb blockade physics. One example is the concurrent use of a single electron transistor (SET) as a charge sensor and as a reservoir for adjacent qubit quantum dots (QD). We find that the SET energy spectrum impacts pulsed gate spectroscopy of the QD when the SET simultaneously acts as the reservoir and the sensor. Then, using the qubit QD as a probe, we quantify how strongly the electron temperature of the SET depends on the applied bias. It surpasses a reference experiment by 100 $mK$ even at 50 $\mu V$ bias. Our results provide a strong incentive for choosing spin qubit initialization schemes which do not rely on coupling to the reservoir in such devices. Switching to a double quantum dot connected to a single reservoir, a frequent configuration for qubit readout, we finally demonstrate the tunability of the characteristic latching behavior by systematically mapping it in the few-electron stability diagram.

### Paper [110]
Title: Designed atomic displacements for finite-temperature phonons from few force calculations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12218
Authors: Albert Linda
Abstract: Finite-temperature phonons are usually fitted to first-principles forces in randomly displaced supercells, and each force calculation can be expensive. We introduce special optimal thermal cells (SOTCs), in which the displacements are designed rather than drawn at random. Our design, SOTC-D, starts from a harmonic trial model. It gives every supercell phonon mode its quantum-thermal amplitude, chooses the sign of each mode so that the force constants are best determined, and pairs each configuration with its inverse so that cubic forces cancel. Updating the trial leads to the self-consistent harmonic solution. Cells that match displacement correlations instead give good thermodynamics but not better dispersions. For eleven crystals, eight force calculations reproduce the effective dispersions within 0.03-0.31 THz, and SOTC-D is the most accurate of five designs in 9 of them. After one trial update, it beats random sampling in all crystals at 4-16 calculations and two-iteration stochastic sampling (sTDEP) in all at eight (supercell basis). On model potentials, the self-consistent form needs 2-16 times fewer calculations than the stochastic self-consistent harmonic approximation in 13 of 14 cases. With the HSE06 hybrid functional, it is ahead of pooled sTDEP at 4-16 calculations in Si and NaCl, and it gives the thermal expansion of Al within 3% of experiment at 300K. The method has limits: in Al and Si cells of more than 100 atoms, sTDEP is more accurate beyond 4-16 calculations (not so in NaCl up to 16); for dynamically stabilized bcc Zr, the outcome depends on the seed; and above 400 K, the free-energy curvature is not resolved.

### Paper [111]
Title: Topological phases and scattering protected by projective chiral symmetry
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12227
Authors: Th\'eo Torres, Renaud C\^ote, Marc Pachebat, Antonin Coutant
Abstract: The bulk-boundary correspondence states that topological properties of a bulk material guarantee the existence of protected edge modes, oftentimes associated with the attractive property of backscattering immunity. In this Letter, we consider a time reversal invariant classical system with both translation and chiral, or sublattice, symmetries. We show that when these two symmetries anti-commute, the material exhibits a topological phase protected by the same invariant as type D topological superconductors. We realize such a system experimentally and observe the existence of the associated edge modes in an acoustic network. Our measurements are in excellent agreement with theoretical predictions from discrete lattice models. Finally, we show that these edge modes are immune to backscattering for perturbations consisting in two subsequent identical defects.

### Paper [112]
Title: Multichannel Phonon Interconversion Drives 90 Polarization Rotation for Stable Domain Walls in Hafnium Oxide
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12264
Authors: Fatoye Sawyerr, Hamoon Fahrvandi, Pawan Kumar, Zekun Zhang, Shuning Lv, Yue Yu, Xuan Gao, Kang Jia, Shifeng Wen, Zheng Wen, Li-Min Liu, Xiaoli Fan, Jun Hee Lee, Tengfei Cao
Abstract: Polarization rotation is a fundamental mechanism in ferroelectric materials, yet how such rotation arises microscopically in fluorite ferroelectrics and gives rise to distinct domain walls (DW) remains unresolved. Phonon modes offer a microscopic route to such structural transformations, as geometric orientations can modify their frequencies, dispersion relations, and vibrational eigenstates when their characteristic wavelengths become comparable to relevant physical dimensions. Here, by combining condensed phonon mode decomposition with first-principles calculations, we investigate the origin of 90 polarization rotation in the ferroelectric phase of HfO2. Using the cubic parent phase as reference, we show that polarization rotation originates from multiple symmetry equivalent phonon modes of distinct eigenstates at different wavevectors in the Brillouin zone, whose coupling and cooperative reorientation continuously evolve to generate distinct 90 Pca21 variants and switching pathways. Among the 90 variants, the lowest-energy switching pathway through the Pmn21 exhibits an energy barrier comparable to, but slightly lower than, that of the 180 polarization reversal pathway through the P42/nmc phase, indicating that 90 polarization rotation is an energetically accessible alternative switching route. We further analyze 90 DW and find that frustrated phonon modes emerge at the wall center, where the coexistence and competition of condensed modes promote local reconfiguration of the lattice distortions, contributing to the elevated DW energies. Importantly, experimental observations with STEM imaging provide direct evidence of atomic mode distortions in stable 90 DW, consistent with the theoretically identified low-energy DW. These findings provide the fundamental structural mechanism underlying 90 switching dynamics, competing domain-wall physics, and macroscopic ferroelectric behavior.

### Paper [113]
Title: Defects and Structural Evolution in CrSBr: Machine-Learning Potentials for Atomistic Simulations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12267
Authors: Arsalan Hashemi, Daniil Kruklinskii, Xiaoya Chang, Tapio Ala-Nissila, Mikko Karttunen, Arkady V. Krasheninnikov, Mahdi Ghorbani-Asl
Abstract: The layered van der Waals semiconductor CrSBr combines intrinsic magnetic order with semiconducting and optical properties, making it a promising material for multifunctional electronic and photonic applications. Understanding how defects and structural transformations affect its properties under experimentally relevant conditions, however, remains challenging for first-principles simulations due to the relevant length and time scales. Here, we develop and benchmark machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) for the Cr-S-Br system, enabling large-scale atomistic simulations of CrSBr with accuracy approaching that of density functional theory (DFT). The charge-aware variant qNEP achieves near-DFT accuracy in predicting lattice constants, formation enthalpies, vacancy energetics, and the anisotropic pressure response of o-CrSBr. Nudged elastic band calculations reveal that pre-existing S vacancies lower the Cr Frenkel-pair migration barrier by up to 1.1 eV, providing an atomistic mechanism for beam-induced Cr redistribution and structural transformations. Displacement threshold calculations further show that Br is the most susceptible species to electron-beam-induced displacement, identifying the relative ease of removing different atomic species. Together, these results establish a validated computational framework for investigating atomic-scale disorder and structural transformations in CrSBr, providing a basis for defect engineering in materials for future magneto-optoelectronic applications.

### Paper [114]
Title: Positronium resonance of electric monopoles in quantum spin ice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12277
Authors: Jos\'e Maria Cruz, Hidemaro Suwa, Masafumi Udagawa
Abstract: Quantum spin ice attracts considerable attention due to its remarkable low-energy properties, described by the effective gauge theory, which mimics the quantum electrodynamics (QED). The ``effective" QED in quantum spin ice is beyond the ``real" QED in our universe in many respects. Above all, the effective QED hosts two kinds of gapped particles, the electric monopole, in addition to the magnetic monopole. Among the two monopoles, the electric monopole is of purely quantum origin, and its character has not been well resolved. In this work, we focus on the Rokhsar Kivelson point of the quantum dimer model, as the low-energy description of the quantum XXZ model on a pyrochlore lattice, and address the excitation spectrum of the electric monopoles, by combining the quantum Monte Carlo simulation with the stochastic analytical continuation. Most strikingly, the electric monopole spectrum exhibits clear double-peak structure, corresponding to the bound states and scattering states of electric monopoles. The lower-energy peak is attributed to the exciton-like bound state of an electric monopole pair, a signal of positronium in quantum spin ice. We found that the flippable loop operator serves as a local physical observable to excite a pair of electric monopoles, implying that monopoles can be detected through lattice or optical response.

### Paper [115]
Title: Tuning correlated states in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene with magnetic encapsulation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12281
Authors: Cuiju Yu, Yitao Sun, Jose L. Lado
Abstract: Rhombohedral graphene multilayers provide a flexible platform for correlated physics associated with their flat-band electronic structure.While a variety of correlated states appear in these systems as a function of bias and electrostatic doping, promoting specific correlated flavor symmetry breaking states remains a challenge. Here we show that magnetic encapsulation by CrGeTe$_3$ achieves this in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene, by using switchable exchange couplings induced by ferromagnetic substrates. We show that correlations in rhombohedral multilayers at charge neutrality strongly renormalize the exchange proximity splitting,giving rise to collective spin modes whose character is switched by the parallel or antiparallel alignment of the encapsulation. We further show that upon doping of the flat bands, spin triplet $f$-wave superconducting solutions emerge, whose layer and spin selectivity is controlled by the magnetic encapsulation, hosting in-gap excitations at the edges. Our results establish magnetic alignment as a means to control spin symmetry breaking and unconventional superconductivity in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene.

### Paper [116]
Title: Ultrafast Liquid Jet X-ray Total Scattering
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12290
Authors: Adam F. Sapnik, Philip A. Chater, Dean S. Keeble, Tobias A. Bird, John S. O. Evans, Federica Bertolotti, Antonietta Guagliardi, Norberto Masciocchi, Daniel T. Bowron, Lise J. St{\o}ckler, Elodie A. Harbourne, Anders B. Borup, Rebecca S. Silberg, Viktoria A. Kaznelson, Stefanie Punke, Mariia Svyrydenko, Maryna I. Bodnarchuk, Dmitry N. Dirin, Simon C. Boehme, Maksym V. Kovalenko, Bo B. Iversen, Emil S. Bozin, Kirsten M. {\O}. Jensen, Gabriele Ansaldi, Karen Appel, Alexander Gierke, J\"org Hallmann, Aliaksandr Leonau, Anders Madsen, Johannes M\"oller, Jan-Etienne Pudell, Angel Rodriguez-Fernandez, Andreas Schmidt, Roman Shayduk, Cornelius Strohm, Joana Valerio, Ulrike Boesenberg, David A. Keen
Abstract: X-ray free-electron lasers (XFELs) provide the temporal resolution needed to extend total scattering and pair distribution function (PDF) analysis to investigate structural dynamics on femtosecond-to-picosecond timescales. We recently demonstrated that high-quality total scattering data and PDFs can be obtained from individual XFEL pulses; here, we take the next step towards time-resolved studies of structural dynamics in solution. Using the Materials Imaging and Dynamics (MID) instrument at the European XFEL, we obtain total scattering data up to $Q_{\max} = 16.4\,\mathring{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}$ from both conventional capillary samples and, critically, from samples delivered by a liquid jet. Despite the large air and solvent backgrounds inherent to this setup, PDFs suitable for quantitative structural analysis are recovered from liquid dispersions and molecular clusters in solution. We further demonstrate pump-probe total scattering from $\mathrm{CsPbBr}_3$ nanocrystals in a liquid jet, resolving a fluence-dependent photoinduced isotropic lattice strain. Additionally, empirical potential structure refinement (EPSR) of XFEL total scattering data from an aqueous Keggin cluster reveals site-specific hydration environments and enables three-dimensional mapping of the surrounding solvent structure. Together, these advances open the door to tracking not only ultrafast structural changes in solution, but also the evolving solvent environments that govern molecular behaviour and reactivity.

### Paper [117]
Title: Atomically Sharp Magnetic Domain Walls beyond Micromagnetics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12303
Authors: Guokai Liao, Stefan Bl\"ugel
Abstract: Atomically narrow magnetic domain walls can arise in ultrathin magnetic films, van der Waals magnets, and systems with reduced effective exchange stiffness due to competing exchange interactions. As their width approaches the atomic lattice spacing, the continuum approximation underlying micromagnetics becomes increasingly unreliable. We investigate the structure and stability of such domain walls in a one-dimensional nearest-neighbor J-K-D spin-lattice model incorporating Heisenberg exchange, uniaxial anisotropy, and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI). In the absence of DMI, we demonstrate analytically a continuous transition to an atomically sharp easy-axis wall at the critical exchange-to-anisotropy ratio J/K = 3/2, characterized by the softening of a localized Hessian mode. Atomistic spin-dynamics simulations confirm this transition and reveal that fitting discrete wall profiles with the conventional micromagnetic solution, as commonly done in experimental analyses, underestimates the exchange interaction and overestimates the anisotropy. Finite DMI removes the perfectly collinear wall as a stationary configuration, replacing the transition by a smooth crossover toward a strongly localized but canted wall that approaches the collinear easy-axis configuration only asymptotically in the strong-anisotropy limit. We derive an analytical expression for the DMI-induced canting in this regime and demonstrate its strongly enhanced canting response near the critical ratio. These findings establish the limitations of micromagnetic descriptions of atomically narrow domain walls and suggest opportunities for controlling their structure through anisotropy and DMI.

### Paper [118]
Title: Ultrafast Inter-Aggregate Energy Transfer Drives Efficient Charge Generation in Organic Homojunctions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12322
Authors: Shanchao Ouyang, Hang Yuen Ho, Sudhi Mahadevan, Ziyao Yue, Haochen Liu, Zhiqiang Guan, Chun-Sing Lee, Shu Kong So, Sai-Wing Tsang
Abstract: Y6-based homojunction organic photovoltaic devices have recently achieved exceptional power conversion efficiencies, surpassing those of conventional organic photovoltaic materials. Although unusually long exciton diffusion lengths and efficient exciton dissociation have been reported, the fundamental mechanism underlying this outstanding performance remains under debate. Here, we report that structurally distinct aggregates in Y6 thin films exhibit complementary charge-transfer (CT) characters that enable directional energy transfer and enhanced exciton delocalization. Using ultrafast spectroscopy, we identify highly efficient F\"orster resonance energy transfer (FRET) between Y6 aggregates, with an ensemble-averaged efficiency of 65% occurring on a sub-picosecond timescale (<1 ps). This aggregate-mediated energy-transfer pathway drives long-range energy migration in Y6 thin films and accounts for the remarkable external quantum efficiencies observed in homojunction devices, even in the absence of conventional donor-acceptor bulk heterojunction morphologies and despite substantial fractions of weak-CT components. Here, using Y6 as a model non-fullerene acceptor (NFA) system, we show that structurally distinct aggregates coexisting in NFA thin films possess complementary CT characters, which are expected to enable directional energy transfer and enhanced exciton delocalization. We establish a general multi-component FRET framework that quantitatively links molecular aggregation, CT character, and excited-state dynamics in systems with multiple packing motifs. This work provides insight into the development of next-generation high-performance organic electronic materials and devices.

### Paper [119]
Title: The role of preparation protocols in the Mpemba effect under time-delayed Newton cooling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12328
Authors: Andr\'es Santos
Abstract: The Mpemba effect depends on both the relaxation dynamics and the protocol used to prepare the initial states. Here, this dependence is investigated using the time-delayed Newton cooling law, comparing protocols in which two samples are exposed to piecewise-constant bath temperatures before being placed in a common final bath. For protocols with at most two quenches and a common waiting time, exact conditions for the occurrence of the effect are derived. They rule out the Pontus protocol within this model and show that the synchronous two-reservoir protocol maximizes the effect magnitude within the class considered. The robustness of this optimum is then examined by allowing asynchronous quenches or an additional quench for one sample. Numerical optimization of these extensions identifies the synchronous two-reservoir protocol as the maximizing limit in the cases studied. This evidence motivates a conjecture that the same protocol remains optimal for more general bath histories, although that broader statement is not proved.

### Paper [120]
Title: Kagome Flat Bands from Self-Assembled Water on Non-van der Waals 2D Materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12371
Authors: Tom Barnowsky, Madeleine Christie, Anastasiia Nihei, Rico Friedrich
Abstract: The properties of two-dimensional (2D) materials are governed not only by their intrinsic electronic structure but also in particular by their surface chemistry and interactions with the environment. The surfaces of non-van der Waals (non-vdW) 2D materials are intrinsically reactive and may require chemical stabilization under realistic experimental conditions. Here, we perform a systematic first-principles study of water passivation of non-vdW 2D materials and demonstrate that molecular water adsorption is energetically and dynamically favored for a wide range of systems. Rather than dissociating, water molecules frequently remain intact and form adsorbed ordered surface overlayers. These overlayers can adopt a kagome lattice geometry, leading to the emergence of flat electronic bands directly at the valence-band edge. Analysis of the electronic structure reveals a strong intermolecular coupling between neighboring water lone pairs within this kagome topology, while the locked molecular orientations simultaneously produce an electrostatically frustrated surface potential. These findings establish water not only as a stabilizing agent but also as an active element for electronic structure engineering of non-vdW 2D surfaces.

### Paper [121]
Title: Quantum Embedding from Molecular to Crystalline Environments at the Cost of a Gas-Phase Calculation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12399
Authors: Manas Sharma, Marek Sierka
Abstract: We present a direct-space formulation and implementation of quantum density functional theory (DFT) embedding for molecular active subsystems embedded in frozen environments (hosts) of arbitrary dimensionality, from finite molecular hosts to extended systems. The host is computed once without the embedded molecule (the guest) and is represented only by its electron density fitted in an atom-centered auxiliary Gaussian basis together with its nuclear charges with the periodic electrostatics separated into an explicit near-field and a continuous fast-multipole far-field operator. The method allows the removal of host atoms from this representation to create vacancies or other voids without requiring a supercell creation. Analytic nuclear gradients are also implemented, enabling structural relaxation of the active subsystem. We demonstrate the versatility of the approach through applications to adsorbates on periodic surfaces and molecules embedded in bulk solvent environments. For CO on MgO(001), we show that DFT-in-DFT converges rapidly and reproduces the periodic interaction energy within 0.44 kJ/mol while reducing the cost by a factor of 27. Real-time time-dependent embedded DFT (RT-TDDFET) of methylenecyclopropene in periodic water is showcased to produce a ~0.6 eV solvatochromic blue shift, while strong-field propagation of CO2 on hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) yields substrate-induced effects at the cost of a gas-phase propagation. Coupling the embedding scheme to excited-state correlated wave function theory methods such as CC2 yields a solvent shift within 0.01 eV of supermolecular CC2 references, while reducing the computational cost by over 500 times.

### Paper [122]
Title: Discrete time crystals stabilized by bounded measurement noise
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12400
Authors: Pallabi Chatterjee, Tanay Nag, Ranjan Modak
Abstract: Periodically driven clean interacting systems generically heat up, which destroys discrete time-crystalline order. Here we ask whether continuous weak measurement can stabilize such order. We study a long-range interacting spin chain whose local occupation is continuously monitored. Measurements generally induce a transition from volume-law to area-law entanglement. Yet low entanglement alone is not enough: along typical quantum trajectories, the system still reaches a featureless state with no subharmonic response. In contrast, the most-probable trajectory, obtained by suppressing measurement fluctuations, shows robust period doubling. To connect these limits, we introduce a bounded-noise protocol that restricts measurement fluctuations to a finite window. Narrow windows preserve period doubling. Intermediate windows produce coexisting time-crystalline and non-time-crystalline trajectories. Over time, trajectories leave the time-crystalline group one by one, so the order is long lived but finite. Wide windows destroy the order. Our results establish bounded measurement noise, discarding the large-deviation trajectories, as a route to time-crystalline order in clean systems. They also show that the measurement-induced area-law phase is necessary, but not sufficient, for this order.

### Paper [123]
Title: Gate-tunable supercurrent switching in a three-terminal quantum dot transistor
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12415
Authors: David Nov\'ak, Kacper Wrze\'{s}niewski, Filip Krizek, Wolfgang Belzig, Peter Zalom
Abstract: We propose and analyze a three-terminal superconducting transistor switch in which a gate-defined quantum dot region interrupts a flux-controlled loop. The output supercurrent is switched off completely when the two loop arms couple to the dot with equal tunneling strengths and half a flux quantum threads the loop. The device then operates as a symmetric SQUID at half flux. The condition follows from a single geometric factor $\chi$, which reduces the three-terminal problem to an equivalent two-terminal Anderson impurity model and makes the switching mechanism independent of the Coulomb interaction, the dot level position, the total hybridization and temperature. These enter only through a prefactor and thus decide how tolerant the device is to imperfect barrier tuning. Numerical renormalization group calculations across the full coupling parameter space show that the resonance is embedded in a broad region of elevated switching ratio at intermediate hybridization $\Gamma \approx \Delta$, while both weaker and stronger coupling reduce it to a thin isolated line. Finite temperature smooths the underlying singlet-doublet transition and lowers the supercurrent amplitude, but affects the open and closed states comparably and leaves the switching ratio largely intact. Within a zero-bandwidth two-level model, a second dot level is shown to open a leakage channel that grows as the two levels approach in energy. Raising the output barrier and the realistic presence of inter-level Coulomb repulsion, however, suppress the leakage.

### Paper [124]
Title: Lattice dynamical properties and interatomic force constants of transition metal oxide perovskite superlattices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12432
Authors: Xiao-Lin Chen, Jin-Zhu Zhao, Philippe Ghosez
Abstract: In this study, we investigate the construction of efficient model potentials for perovskite heterostructures by transferring relevant dynamic quantities from bulk systems and short-period superlattices. Through first-principles calculations, we provide a comprehensive analysis and comparison of the dynamical properties of different perovskite-based superlattice structures, including (PbTiO3 )n /(SrTiO3 )m superlattices, (ATiO3 )n /(AO) 2 superlattices, and An+1 Tin O 3n+1 Ruddlesden-Popper phases, where A can be Sr, Ba, or Pb. We demonstrate that as the thickness of these structures increases, the dynamical properties of the perovskite blocks converge rapidly towards their bulk counterparts. We discuss this convergence in terms of phonon density of states in reciprocal space and inter-atomic force constants in real space. Our findings suggest a straightforward strategy for developing transferable model potentials that accurately capture the dynamics of layered oxide compounds with progressively thicker layers.

### Paper [125]
Title: Deriving Tsallis Entropy from First Principles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12441
Authors: J. A. S. Lima, M. H. Benetti
Abstract: The kinetic approach for q-distribution is extended through a "bottom-up" procedure to the corresponding entropic formula. In this way, the validity of Tsallis' formula, S_q(W) = k_B ln_q(W), is established from first principles. The resulting q-entropy is additive and the zeroth law of thermodynamics is automatically satisfied because the conventional identification beta = 1/(k_B T) remains valid. Entropy, energy, and all relevant quantities for both discrete and continuous cases are evaluated using standard (linear) averages. The "bottom-up" and "top-down" approaches yield the same q-distribution for velocities. The standard results are naturally recovered in the limit q -> 1.

### Paper [126]
Title: Real Topology on Symmetry-Invariant Loops Enforces Superconducting Pairing Zeros
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12454
Authors: Hongyu Lu, Hao Jin, Wang Yao
Abstract: Normal-state topology can obstruct superconducting pairing and give rise to gapless nodes in the quasiparticle spectrum. However, existing pairing-obstruction mechanisms largely rely on Fermi-surface topology or global Bloch-band topology. Here, we establish a new and general mechanism: one-dimensional (1D) real topology, defined on antiunitary-symmetry-invariant loops, enforces pairing zeros without requiring Fermi-surface or global Bloch-band topology. We derive sufficient conditions and lower bounds for pairing zeros across diverse pairing channels, for both zero and finite Cooper-pair momenta. The theorems apply to systems of arbitrary spatial dimensions without requiring the pairing bands to be globally isolated. We further show that such topologically enforced pairing zeros, if isolated, necessarily carry odd vorticity and can give rise to Bogoliubov-de Gennes nodes and zero-energy boundary modes, as demonstrated in a minimal two-orbital model. Our results reshape the understanding of how normal-state topology can obstruct the superconducting order, highlight the significant role of 1D real topology beyond single-particle band phenomena, and provide a general framework for identifying candidate materials hosting nodal superconductivity.

### Paper [127]
Title: Revealing laser-induced modification of weak ferromagnetism through a spin-reorientation phase transition in iron borate Fe$_3$BO$_6$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12460
Authors: A. V. Kuzikova, S. N. Barilo, R. A. Lanovsky, A. V. Sadovnikov, R. V. Pisarev, A. M. Kalashnikova
Abstract: In the iron oxide Fe$_3$BO$_6$ weak ferromagnetism stems from competing contributions from two-site Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions (DMI) and from on-site anisotropies at the two $4c$ and $8d$ types of nonequivalent Fe$^{3+}$ sites. As a result, spin reorientation (SR) transition $\Gamma_2$ $\rightarrow$ $\Gamma_4$ in Fe$_3$BO$_6$ uniquely comprises 90$^\circ$ spin switching and a 35% change of weak ferromagnetic moment. We explore ultrafast kinetics of such a transition as a proxy for weak ferromagnetism contributions modified by femtosecond laser pulses. Using magneto-optical pump-probe technique we reveal that the SR transition is triggered by an abrupt decrease of the weak ferromagnetic moment in the $\Gamma_2$ phase, and relate it to the laser-induced modification of the DMI and anisotropy contributions to the spin canting. By tuning the pump pulse phonon energy between different optical absorption ranges, we show that laser-driven impact on spin canting possesses a nonthermal character and is achieved through the charge-transfer transitions $2p\rightarrow3d$ within Fe$^{3+}$O$^{2-}_6$ octahedra rather than by localized $3d\rightarrow3d$ transitions at Fe$^{3+}$. The decreased weak ferromagnetic moment controls the threshold and the latency of the SR transition, as well as a final state. The latter is achieved via the precessional switching to the $\Gamma_4$ phase activated by the laser-induced heating. Final state is found to be a coexistence of $\Gamma_4$ and $\Gamma_2$ phase with modified spin canting persisting even at hundreds of picoseconds.

### Paper [128]
Title: Giant Diffusion at Flocking Transitions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12465
Authors: Sohini Chatterjee, Wasim Akram, Subir K. Das
Abstract: We simulate dynamics in two-dimensional aligning active-matter models exhibiting order-disorder transitions in the velocity field. Analysis reveals that the single-particle diffusivity, $D_s$, exhibits strong enhancements at criticality which we quantify via a finite-size scaling method. This dynamical behavior at nonequilibrium flocking transitions is fundamentally different from that at the criticality of equilibrium systems, where diffusivities either vanish or remain non-critical. Furthermore, $D_s$ exhibits pronounced finite-size effects throughout the ordered phase, which also are absent in equilibrium settings. We interpret these counterintuitive transport anomalies within the framework of a flocking theory. Our results suggest that enhanced diffusion generates effective long-range interactions. This relaxes the constraints of the Mermin-Wagner theorem that otherwise prevents order-disorder transitions in two dimensions for systems possessing continuous symmetry. These results are of much experimental relevance and have important implications in formulating quantitative theories for nonequilibrium transports.

### Paper [129]
Title: Scalable operating currents in amorphous W-N/FePt superconducting rectifiers
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12476
Authors: S. Russo, A. Sanson, Yeonkyu Lee, Jeehoon Kim, N. Haberkorn
Abstract: We demonstrate high-efficiency, field-free superconducting diode behavior in W-N/FePt bilayers fabricated entirely by sputtering at room temperature. The devices combine amorphous W-N superconducting thin films with a 48-nm-thick FePt layer exhibiting remanent stripe-domain magnetization. We used lithographically patterned microbridges to compare W-N/FePt bilayers with single-layer W-N reference films at three nominal W-N thicknesses (18, 24, and 28 nm). The bilayers exhibit superconducting transition temperatures of approximately 4.1-4.3 K. At zero applied magnetic field, the current-voltage (I-V) characteristics exhibit pronounced nonreciprocity, controlled by the orientation of the in-plane remanent FePt magnetization relative to the transport current. The diode response is maximized for perpendicular alignment, reverses polarity upon magnetization reversal, and is strongly suppressed when the magnetization is aligned with the current. This behavior is consistent with asymmetric vortex entry and motion governed by the magnetic stray fields generated near the bridge edges. At 3 K with the remanent magnetization perpendicular to the current, increasing the W-N thickness from 18 to 28 nm widens the current interval between the dissipation thresholds for opposite polarities from about 0.3 to 1.55 mA. All three bilayers exhibit diode efficiencies of approximately 75-80% in this configuration.

### Paper [130]
Title: Schottky barrier height modulation and memristive behavior in a mechanically exfoliated $MoS_2$ flake with multiple lateral electrodes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12484
Authors: Gabriel Caballero, Ram\'on Bernardo-Gavito, Mariela Menghini, Daniel Granados
Abstract: Van de Waals materials possess great potential as core active materials for novel electronics, optoelectronics, and sensing applications. In particular, transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) and other low-dimensional materials, such as hexagonal boron nitride and binary oxides exhibit memristive behavior, making them attractive candidates as building blocks for resistive memories and neuromorphic computing devices. In this work, we investigate two-terminal lateral memristors based on mechanically exfoliated few layer $MoS_2$ flakes. We study the influence of the Schottky barrier height (SBH) as a function of the contact area. We report different SBHs from 60 meV up to 208 meV in the same $MoS_2$ flake and analyze the effect of the SBH in the memristive-like $J-V_{DS}$ curves and the observed non-volatile/volatile resistance switching. Our results highlight the importance of contact engineering to obtain different memristive properties in $MoS_2$-based devices.

### Paper [131]
Title: Holonomy, Spectral Topology, and Detailed Balance in Classical and Open Quantum Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12514
Authors: Alvan Hunt
Abstract: Band topology and non-Hermitian spectral theory are now applied to mechanical metamaterials, chiral active fluids, stochastic networks, and open quantum systems. We give a tutorial synthesis organized by holonomy and by one question: what can the spectral data of a linear generator reveal about detailed balance? Three holonomies recur: the Berry phase of a band, the Burgers vector of a dislocation (the integrated torsion of the defect geometry), and the cycle affinity of a Markov network (the holonomy of the connection $\ln(W_{ij}/W_{ji})$). We rederive the Chern numbers $C_m=m(\mathrm{sgn}\,\Omega+\mathrm{sgn}\,\nu_o)$ of a compressible odd-viscous fluid and restate the dislocation zero-mode count of Paulose, Chen, and Vitelli as a pairing of integrated torsion with topological polarization. Our central message is that spectral topology can obstruct detailed balance but does not determine the magnitude of dissipation. Detailed balance is self-adjointness in the inner product fixed by the stationary state, so a non-real eigenvalue, an exceptional point (EP), or a nonzero point-gap winding number of a Markov generator rules it out and forces entropy production $\sigma>0$, with $\sigma\ge k_B j^2/D$ for any current, whereas a real, diagonalizable spectrum does not in general certify it. In the Hatano-Nelson ring, which is normal yet has complex spectrum, $\sigma$ sweeps $(0,\infty)$ at fixed winding number. EPs need a separate assessment of the dynamics: with generalized detailed balance including time-reversal parity, an EP is compatible with equilibrium only through time-reversal-odd dynamics, as at critical damping of a Langevin oscillator, whereas an EP of a system with only even variables, such as an odd elastic medium, certifies a nonequilibrium steady state; in both cases $\sigma$ is analytic across the EP. We propose benchtop tests for each statement.

### Paper [132]
Title: Topological states in fractals through a self-similar tensor network encoding
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12546
Authors: Zebedeus F. Osseweijer, Jagoda Malag, Jose L. Lado, Tiago V. C. Ant\~ao, Anouar Moustaj
Abstract: Tensor network methods have been instrumental in addressing exponentially large many-body problems. Most recently, these techniques have also been applied to single-particle tight-binding Hamiltonians, allowing observables to be evaluated on exponentially large lattices. Here, we show that the scale-separated nature of these methods allows for a natural encoding of self-similar structures, such as the Sierpi\'nski gasket, a scale-invariant fractal with Hausdorff dimension $\log 3/\log 2 \approx 1.58$, yielding an exact and compact representation of Hamiltonians on such lattices. Using this encoding, we study a Chern insulator, the flux kagome model, confined to this fractal geometry, and develop methods to investigate its topological states within a tensor network framework. This allows us to reach much higher fractal generations than would otherwise be possible with dense-matrix methods, resolving the density of states of a deep fractal with over 100 million sites. Furthermore, the larger structures allow us to resolve topology in smaller gaps, suggesting that these methods could be instrumental in understanding the interplay between topology and non-integer dimensionality.

### Paper [133]
Title: Memory in biological L\'evy walks from the dynamic-entropy formalism
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12547
Authors: Adriano Barra, Andrea Ladiana
Abstract: L\'evy walks are recognized by the histogram of their flight lengths, while the renewal hypothesis on which the textbook model rests, that every flight is drawn afresh, is seldom tested. We test it with the dynamic-entropy formalism. Maximizing the causal entropy of a sequence of logarithmic flight lengths at fixed lagged correlations yields a chain of continuous spins on a compact interval, whose depth, selected by the data, tells a renewal walker from a Markovian one and from one with a longer memory. With one lag the chain is exactly solvable. A duality that exchanges long and short flights locates a first-order condensation of the flights at the upper cutoff, with the logarithmic range of the flights in the role of system size, and a positive memory makes the histogram heavier-tailed than the rule that generates each flight. For groups, the same construction separates the memory of each individual from delayed interactions and from drives common to all units, which an analysis of the group average confounds, and it gives the collective speed a critical point. On open data, renewal is rejected in seven of ten conditions of Drosophila larvae and in every termite, whose memory reaches a few flights back and lowers the exponent of the histogram by more than half a unit below that of the one-step rule. Wild baboons align with where their nearest neighbors, those ahead above all, were heading 10 to 30 s earlier, and homing pigeons are coupled across their small flocks. Zebrafish T cells, a negative control, remember their own speed, but no delayed coupling between the speeds of different cells survives the controls.

### Paper [134]
Title: Direct imaging of vortex lattices across fractional fillings in two-dimensional Josephson junction arrays
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12548
Authors: Cequn Li, Austin R. Kaczmarek, Omri Lesser, Maciej W. Olszewski, Katja C. Nowack
Abstract: Josephson junction arrays are predicted to host a rich sequence of commensurate vortex lattices. However, direct imaging of these configurations remains limited, while transport measurements cannot resolve the underlying vortex arrangements or how order breaks down. Using scanning superconducting quantum interference device (SQUID) microscopy, we resolve individual vortices and directly image this long-predicted hierarchy spanning filling factors $f$ = 1/8 to 1/2 in two-dimensional Au-Nb Josephson junction arrays, including several states with no discernible transport signatures. At $f$ = 1/4, two structurally distinct vortex orders with 4$\times$4 and 2$\times$4 unit cells coexist, consistent with their energetic degeneracy in the frustrated XY model. Away from commensurate filling, we resolve defect structures tied to the underlying vortex-lattice order: the $f$ = 1/2 checkerboard retains its order around isolated excess vortices and vacancies, whereas the $f$ = 1/3 staircase fragments into domains separated by domain walls. These results reveal how frustration, competing configurations, and defects shape the spatial coherence of commensurate vortex order in engineered superconducting lattices.

### Paper [135]
Title: Resolving the backbone of multiscale interactions through mutual information rates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12554
Authors: Alberto Raiola, Giorgio Nicoletti, Daniel Maria Busiello
Abstract: Natural systems are often composed of processes evolving on multiple timescales and coupled through complex interaction networks. Inferring such networks is especially challenging when timescales are widely separated, since resolving the fastest processes while accurately sampling the slowest ones is generally prohibitive. In this work, we propose a framework to reconstruct a multiscale interaction tree by combining a rescaled version of the mutual information rate, its conditional counterpart, and the ordering of timescales. We derive an exact theory for stationary Gaussian processes, finding that these measures identify directly connected processes, with the asymptotic mutual information rate distinguishing fast-to-slow from slow-to-fast couplings. We show that this reconstruction can be achieved from the spectral matrix and illustrate its robustness to finite trajectory lengths, moderate timescale separation, and weak nonlinearities. Our work paves the way for exploiting the presence of multiple timescales in real-world systems as a source of structural identifiability rather than an obstacle to network inference.

### Paper [136]
Title: Quotient Index for Symmetry-Protected Zeros and the Tenfold Way in Mackey's Construction of Super Corepresentations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12561
Authors: Zheng Zhang, Y. X. Zhao
Abstract: Symmetries that anticommute with a Hamiltonian can protect zero modes by obstructing their removal through symmetry-compatible perturbations. We formulate this obstruction as a quotient of the additive group of symmetry representations by the image of the map that forgets a compatible representation grading. To compute the quotient, we use Mackey's method to construct all irreducible super corepresentations of finite graded symmetry groups, allowing antiunitary operations and projective factor systems. Starting from the unitary Hamiltonian-preserving normal subgroup, this construction yields two complex and ten real structural types, characterized by residual Altland--Zirnbauer symmetry algebras. The residual and induced graded modules have isomorphic graded commutants, and their represented symmetry algebras share a graded Morita class that is super opposite to that of the commutant. We derive character criteria that identify these types. Exhaustive enumeration of the restriction relations gives independent sector contributions $0$, $Z$, or $Z_2$, with only integer factors in the purely unitary case. In the finite semisimple setting, the complete index vanishes if and only if all zero modes can be lifted by a symmetry-compatible perturbation. Applications to one-dimensional end states and interpolation cylinders of disordered boundary Dirac theories recover protected multiplet counts and connect the quotient to bulk topology and sigma-model topological terms using bulk--boundary correspondence, texture-index theorems, and fermion-phase calculations.

### Paper [137]
Title: Quantum benchmarks for magnetic energies and transition temperatures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12566
Authors: Mojtaba Alaei, Ilia Mikhailov, Alireza Qaiumzadeh, Artem Oganov
Abstract: Predicting magnetic ordering temperatures requires accurate exchange interactions and an appropriate treatment of spin thermodynamics. Here we assess these requirements through complementary quantum Monte Carlo benchmarks. For five insulating antiferromagnets, we compare ferromagnetic-antiferromagnetic energy differences from diffusion Monte Carlo (DMC) and density functional theory with references reconstructed from inelastic neutron scattering (INS) exchanges. DMC underestimates these references but achieves a mean absolute error comparable to the best-performing functionals tested. Separately, we directly test the previously proposed (S+1)/S rescaling of classical Monte Carlo temperatures against quantum stochastic series expansion simulations for 17 compounds using corresponding INS-derived Heisenberg Hamiltonians. Rescaling reduces the mean absolute classical-quantum temperature difference from 81.7 to 8.2 K, with a mean absolute percentage difference of 5.9 %. These results establish a direct quantum benchmark for the rescaling within the tested models and emphasize that improvements in electronic magnetic energy scales should be accompanied by attention to quantum spin thermodynamics.

### Paper [138]
Title: Contrasting one- and two-particle excitations in cuprate strange metals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12569
Authors: Falk R\"oder. Roman Schuster, Martin Knupfer, Axel Lubk, J\"org Fink
Abstract: In unconventional superconductivity, the pairing mechanism remains one of the central unresolved problems in solid-state physics. Moreover, the normal state shows strong deviations from conventional Fermi-liquid behavior, most notably a scattering rate that is linear in energy and temperature. Here we study the momentum dependence of the energy and damping of collective excitations in optimally doped cuprates in the normal state. In particular, we analyze existing Electron Energy Loss Spectroscopy (EELS) data of Bi2Sr2CaCu2O8 [Phys. Rev. B 44, 7155 (1991)] and new analogous optical plasmon data of Bi2Sr2Ca2Cu3O10. Furthermore, we reanalyze similar Resonant Inelastic Xray Scattering (RIXS) acoustic plasmon data [Phys. Rev. R 6, 043184 (2024)] of La1.84Sr0.16CuO4 and La1.84Ce0.16CuO4. We contrast these two-particle excitation results with one-particle excitations measured by Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES). Most of the plasmon behavior can be described well by combined self-energies of a hole and an electron. The dispersion of optical plasmons at high energy is only weakly determined by correlation effects, indicating resilient quasiparticles. The damping of the optical plasmons at low energy, however, is strongly influenced by the reduced scattering rates described in the marginal Fermi liquid model for "strange-metal" behavior. In addition, the same model describes the linear-in-energy damping of acoustic plasmons. Contrary to some theoretical predictions, we see no overdamped optical plasmons below the classical Lindhard critical wave vector.

### Paper [139]
Title: Nonlinear integral equations for stress-controlled rheology: Collocation methods for Volterra equations with functional kernels
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12578
Authors: Stephan Domann, Thomas Voigtmann
Abstract: We present a class of numerical collocation methods to solve a typical inverse problem that arises in the stress-controlled rheology of complex fluids with pronounced history-dependent response, where the relation between stress and flow rate is given by an integral constitutive equation as the material law. We demonstrate the method by calculating the creep response of a nontrivial model of a shear-thinning viscoelastic fluid where a stress overshoot in rate-controlled startup flow leads to ``sudden yielding'' in stress-controlled creep. Our method is able to address the long-time behavior of the model, distinguishing the amorphous solid from the stress-induced yielded fluid, and from the in-between case of infinite deformation that never achieves a stationary shear rate (proper creep). To this end, we implement a method with flexible time-stepping, and prove uniqueness of the solution and convergence of the methods for a broad class of highly viscoelastic non-linear rheological laws in integral form.

### Paper [140]
Title: Spin-valve effect through electrostatic coupling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12588
Authors: Jan Phillips, Joaqu\'in Fern\'andez-Rossier
Abstract: We propose a spin-valve effect in which an insulating magnetic van der Waals multilayer controls the conductance of an adjacent non-magnetic conductor by electrostatic coupling, without transport-electrons entering the magnetic layers. The coupling is induced by the variations of the electronic structure when the relative orientation of the magnetization of adjacent monolayers changes from parallel to antiparallel, modifying both the dielectric constant and band alignment. We consider two types of coupling and device concepts: charge transfer to a graphene monolayer and capacitive coupling in a single-electron transistor. We use density functional calculations to assess the magnitude of the effect in various materials, including CrI$_3$, CrBr$_3$, CrCl$_3$ and NiBr$_2$. We predict magnetoresistance ratios in the range of 1.14-1.20 for the charge-transfer device and up to $10^3$ in the single-electron transistor.

### Paper [141]
Title: Harmonic origin of local symmetry breaking in PbTe revealed using machine-learned potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12598
Authors: Seongjoo Jung, Nicola A. Spaldin
Abstract: We use machine-learned interatomic potentials combined with higher-order interatomic force constant models to show that finite-range polar correlations in PbTe are driven by long-range harmonic interactions rather than lattice anharmonicity. Our molecular dynamics simulations reveal a spatial polar correlation range exceeding 50 {\AA} at low temperatures and decreasing with increasing temperature. Selective interatomic force constant mapping demonstrates that crystal anharmonicity usually associated with the formation of polar nanoregions suppresses polar correlations. Instead, long-range harmonic interactions extending to 13 {\AA} are necessary to reproduce the spatial coherence of local dipoles. In reciprocal space, these extended harmonic interactions correspond to high curvature of the transverse optical (TO) phonon branch at the $\Gamma$ point. Comparison with isostructural NaCl connects these extended interactions to local underscreening of predominantly Coulombic forces. Our results provide a key microscopic description of finite-range polar correlations and a framework for understanding local symmetry breaking in centrosymmetric materials.

### Paper [142]
Title: GibbScope: interactive calculation and visualization of phase diagrams in the browser
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12616
Authors: Ramya Ramachandran, Zhuocheng Huang, Jiayang Wang, Marat I. Latypov
Abstract: Phase diagrams are a constant work in progress as Gibbs-energy models are fitted, revised, and disputed in the light of an endless stream of experimental and computational data. Yet, phase diagrams are still largely communicated as static figures, in much the same way as a century ago. We present GibbScope, a web-based framework for interactive calculation and visualization of binary phase diagrams. Written in Rust and compiled to WebAssembly, the new CALPHAD engine recomputes a complete phase diagram in the browser in a fraction of a second with zero installation on the user or server side. The high speed allows new ways of interacting with phase diagrams and underlying assessments. Validation against well-established CALPHAD software shows agreement to within 0.01 K for invariant temperatures and within 1e-6 for tie-line compositions for most databases. GibbScope offers interactive CALPHAD parameter adjustments, overlays of different assessments and point-by-point misfit to experimental data, which makes the diagram a live, working object. In addition, GibbScope provides convenient access to 2745 thermodynamic databases from the SGTE collection (Hallstedt, 2025) and their live adjustment. For over 1200 of these databases, all phases are modeled in full with the current implementation.

### Paper [143]
Title: Compensation Domain Walls in Ferrimagnets as Spin-Torque Oscillator Candidates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12652
Authors: Matteo Fettizio, Can Onur Avci
Abstract: Spin-torque nano-oscillators are promising nanoscale microwave sources, yet their performance is often limited by narrow operational ranges and sensitivity to device parameters. Here, we numerically investigate the dynamics of compensation domain walls in ferrimagnetic systems driven by spin-transfer torque as a microwave source candidate. These walls arise in structures with adjacent regions of different compensation character, where the competition between exchange and Zeeman energies under an applied magnetic field stabilizes a distinct magnetic texture. We demonstrate steady current-driven rotation of compensation domain walls over a broad range of current densities within the model. To elucidate the underlying mechanism, we develop a collective-coordinate model and derive an analytical expression for the oscillation frequency, which quantitatively reproduces the dependence on current and magnetic field observed in micromagnetic simulations. Our results identify compensation domain walls as a candidate platform for tunable spin-torque nano-oscillators with intrinsic field-induced confinement.

### Paper [144]
Title: A Hierarchy of Fast and Strong Signatures for Vertex Orbits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12653
Authors: Joe Germany, Mohamad Al Sayed, Rana Dbouk, Amer E. Mouawad, Sara Najem
Abstract: The defining features of a physical system are its symmetries and its invariants. For a network, the symmetries are the automorphisms, and the invariants are the vertex orbits they define. Two vertices of a graph may appear structurally indistinguishable without being interchangeable under any graph automorphism. Distinguishing apparent similarity from genuine symmetry lies at the heart of the vertex-orbit partition problem. In this work, we develop a hierarchy of increasingly expressive, automorphism-invariant signatures that draw on the spectral properties of the graph Laplacian and the Dirac operator to reveal subtle structural differences. Guided by a sequence of counterexamples, we progressively strengthen these signatures, ultimately incorporating probing techniques, which are temporary structural perturbations, that expose the hidden distinctions in the original unperturbed graph. We evaluate our approach across diverse benchmark families, recovering exact orbit partitions in challenging instances, including graphs that resist low-dimensional Weisfeiler--Leman refinement. We also examine the computational and numerical limitations of our constructions, distinguishing empirical success from theoretical completeness. Our findings highlight the potential of spectral and probing-based methods for uncovering the structural distinctions that define vertex orbit.

### Paper [145]
Title: Higher tensor network states in two spatial dimensions: fundamental theorem for open boundary conditions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12658
Authors: Clement Delcamp, Boris Kj{\ae}r
Abstract: We introduce a class of two-dimensional tensor network states obtained by categorifying matrix product states. This procedure leads us to consider bond spaces provided by a 2-vector space associated with the 2-cells of the underlying lattice. Tensor network representations are then defined as module categories over the free monoidal category on a monoidal signature encoding the lattice geometry. We prove a fundamental theorem stating that two representations generating the same states with suitable open boundary conditions are equivalent as module categories. Finally, we introduce a notion of 2-injectivity, a categorified analogue of the injectivity condition for matrix product states.

### Paper [146]
Title: Ground-state phase diagram of the spin-1/2 Heisenberg zigzag ladder with anisotropic $\Gamma$ term revisited
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12669
Authors: Toshiya Hikihara, Nobuya Maeshima, Kouichi Okunishi
Abstract: We study the spin-1/2 Heisenberg antiferromagnet with an anisotropic $\Gamma$ term on a zigzag ladder, which can be exactly mapped to the spin-1/2 XYZ zigzag ladder by a spin rotation. This model has recently attracted considerable interest as an effective model for the quasi-one-dimensional magnetic semiconductor YbCuS$_2$. For the model, the ground-state phase diagram was previously proposed by Saito and Hotta [Phys. Rev. Lett. 132, 166701 (2024); Phys. Rev. B 110, 024409 (2024)]. We systematically reexamine the phase diagram through complementary numerical and analytical approaches, including exact diagonalization, the density-matrix renormalization group method, and low-energy effective theories. We thereby demonstrate that when the anisotropic $\Gamma$ term is present, the model exhibits the N\'{e}el, dimer, ferromagnetic, and double-N\'{e}el phases, all of which have a finite excitation gap. The gapless Tomonaga-Luttinger-liquid (TLL) phase appears only in the isotropic case where the $\Gamma$ term is absent. We also confirm that the transitions between the N\'{e}el and dimer phases and between the N\'{e}el and ferromagnetic phases are continuous transitions governed by Gaussian criticality with central charge $c=1$. The TLL parameter characterizing the critical states at the Gaussian transitions is estimated numerically.

### Paper [147]
Title: Renewal Bias in Parallel Simulations of Absorbing Processes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12692
Authors: Sandro M. Reia, Jos\'e F. Fontanari
Abstract: Asynchronous parallel simulations of stochastic processes with absorbing states can exhibit systematic sampling bias when trajectory durations depend on their eventual absorbing outcomes. Workers that continuously restart upon absorption preferentially record rapidly terminating trajectories within a fixed computational budget, distorting the observed ensemble statistics. We investigate this effect using a minimal renewal model with two absorbing states and state-dependent exponential durations. Renewal theory yields exact expressions for the expected number and composition of completed trajectories, showing that the resulting bias decreases algebraically with execution time but persists as the number of parallel workers increases at fixed budget. We compare continuous sampling with First-Trajectory Filtering, which retains only the initial trajectory attempted by each worker. Although finite execution deadlines introduce residual censoring bias, this bias decreases exponentially with the computational budget. An analytical mean-squared error calculation reveals budget-dependent crossover worker allocations beyond which First-Trajectory Filtering becomes more accurate despite collecting fewer trajectories. We also examine the limitations of statistical inference under severe censoring when the absorbing-state probabilities and characteristic durations are unknown. Finally, simulations of the Axelrod model near its nonequilibrium transition demonstrate the preferential sampling of rapidly absorbing fragmented configurations, illustrating the relevance of the renewal mechanism in a spatially interacting system. Our results show that increasing computational throughput does not necessarily improve statistical accuracy and highlight the importance of accounting for trajectory-duration heterogeneity in parallel simulations of absorbing processes.

### Paper [148]
Title: Spin Shuttling Through Quantum-Dot Based Chiral Molecular Bridges: Gauge Constraints, Holonomy, and Quantum Gates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12720
Authors: Amitoj S. Miglani (Department of Physics, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee, India), Rajdeep R. Dwivedi (Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee, India), Balsant S. Tiwari (University of Birmingham, United Kingdom), Vishvendra S. Poonia (Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee, India)
Abstract: Chiral molecular bridges between silicon quantum dots can provide novel two-qubit entangling resources via the chirality-induced spin-selectivity (CISS) effect. We evaluate this using Makhlin invariants. Modeling a phase-coherent bridge as an $\mathrm{SU}(2)$-valued hopping between two spins, a Schrieffer-Wolff reduction yields a twisted exchange interaction $J \mathbf{S}_1 \cdot \mathbf{R} \mathbf{S}_2$, whose Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya and symmetric anisotropic parts are fixed by a single bridge angle $\alpha$. Using the Kaplan-Shekhtman-Entin-Wohlman-Aharony (KSEA) gauge argument, we prove this interaction is locally equivalent to isotropic exchange; thus, a single chiral bond does not constitute a new entangling class. Breaking this gauge symmetry reveals genuine chiral utility: chiral exchange converts a uniform transverse field into a synthetic gradient, bringing the two-qubit propagator within $7\times10^{-4}$ of the CNOT class in invariant space 4.7 times faster than an equivalent orthogonal gradient. Shuttling a spin through bridges with non-collinear axes implements a non-Abelian $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ holonomy, yielding rotationally non-reciprocal transport and closed-loop geometric gates that suppress dynamical-phase noise to second order. To address the unsettled microscopic origin of CISS, we introduce a unified transport channel combining coherent rotation, a direction-reversing spin filter, and pointer-basis dephasing. This model obeys an exact $\tanh$ composition law for pumped polarization and a closed-form transverse decay law, yielding four observables that experimentally over-determine the bridge's coherent fraction $F_c$. Finally, we quantify silicon-specific hardware constraints, establishing the Landau-Zener valley-adiabaticity window ($E_v \geq 2.1\sqrt{\hbar v}$) and charge-noise limit ($\sigma_J/J \leq 3\%$) required for $99\%$ gate fidelity.

### Paper [149]
Title: Out-of-plane interference effects in the angle-resolved photoemission of epitaxial graphene
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12733
Authors: E. Favorite, P. Kr\"uger, Ph. Rosenzweig, R. Joshi, S. Williams, B. Matta, K. K\"uster, M. Hashimoto, U. Starke, H. Nakamura
Abstract: The epitaxial growth of monolayer graphene by Si sublimation of 6H-SiC(0001) is one of the most effective ways of producing large-scale monocrystalline graphene samples of high quality. However, graphene is left interacting with a reconstructed carbon buffer layer known as zerolayer graphene (ZLG) and the SiC substrate underneath. Beyond the mere creation of Dirac cone replicas, the effect of this interaction on photoemission measurements is poorly understood. We utilized synchrotron radiation for photon-energy-dependent angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) to investigate the complex final-state interactions of photoelectrons within the heterostructure of monolayer graphene, ZLG, and SiC. We identify multiple resonances in the photoemission intensity of the Dirac cone as a function of photon energy that originate from interlayer interference, confirmed with a multiple scattering model. We also demonstrate for the first time that the relative intensity of the replica bands induced by the $(6\sqrt{3} \times 6\sqrt{3})\text{R}30^\circ$ reconstruction scales with the electron kinetic energy. Finally, we resolved four nondispersing electronic states, which originate from the ZLG. Our findings show that the three-dimensional (3D) structure of SiC and ZLG manifests itself in the photoemission from two-dimensional (2D) epitaxial graphene. By revealing how 3D substrate architecture dictates the photoemission of a 2D layer, this work establishes a crucial framework for investigating the electronic structure of complex heterostructures via ARPES.

### Paper [150]
Title: Catalysis-induced phase separation of enzymes via diffusivity contrast
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12735
Authors: Marie Jardat, Jacques Fries, Vincent Dahirel, Jaime Agudo-Canalejo, Pierre Illien
Abstract: Catalysis can drive phase separation by coupling local chemical composition to molecular transport, even in the absence of attractive interactions. Here, we demonstrate such catalysis-induced phase separation in a microscopic particle-based model of enzymes catalyzing the interconversion of substrate and product molecules with different diffusivities. We derive an analytical instability criterion in the dilute limit, showing that a homogeneous mixture becomes unstable when the nonequilibrium chemical drive and the diffusivity contrast are sufficiently large, and the substrate and product concentration exceeds a threshold set by the enzyme volume. Brownian-dynamics simulations confirm the predicted instability and reveal macroscopic coexistence of enzyme-rich and enzyme-poor phases, including in regimes where equilibrium depletion interactions cannot account for demixing. Finally, we show that, upon eliminating the fast chemical degrees of freedom, the reactive ternary mixture maps onto an effective binary mixture nonreciprocal interactions. Our results establish an explicit microscopic mechanism for catalysis-induced phase separation and connect chemically driven phase separation to the physics of motility-regulated and nonreciprocal matter.

### Paper [151]
Title: Finite temperature and pressure structural relaxations including zero-point motion and anharmonicity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12736
Authors: Henricus R. A. ten Eikelder, Florian Knoop, Florian Trybel
Abstract: At finite temperature and pressure, the equilibrium structure of a crystal minimizes the Gibbs free energy rather than the static Born-Oppenheimer energy. We present WARP (Wyckoff-constrained Anharmonic Relaxations at Pressure and temperature), a symmetry-constrained, variable-cell relaxation based on the stochastic temperature-dependent effective potential method, including anharmonicity and zero-point motion. Every position update is projected onto Wyckoff positions and every strain onto symmetric strains, so a relaxation can raise the symmetry but never lower it. For aluminum, the thermal expansion follows measurements at cryogenic temperatures, including the quantum plateau below 50 K. For lanthanum hydrides at megabar pressures, equations of state agree with phonon-based and path-integral calculations, and LaH$_{10}$ relaxes from its rhombohedral static minimum to the observed cubic phase, also with a machine-learned potential fine-tuned on 150 supercells. For brucite, Mg(OD)$_2$, lattice parameters, bond distances and thermal expansion agree with neutron diffraction, and the O-D isotope effect is captured.

### Paper [152]
Title: Universal semiclassical drift velocity of magnetic edge channels
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12783
Authors: Ekaterina R. Sleptsova, Alexey A. Sokolik
Abstract: Magnetic edge channels, arising at the edge of a two-dimensional electron gas in a magnetic field, are topologically protected and propagate unidirectionally along the edge. Their group velocities at the Fermi level, being averaged harmonically, result in drift velocity, which is related to the edge density of states, quantum capacitance, and the velocity of edge magnetoplasmons. Using semiclassical quantization of electron skipping orbits, we show that the drift velocity equals 1/2 of the electron Fermi velocity in the limit of a large number of edge channels crossing the Fermi level. This result, being universal for a hard edge and for isotropic dispersion, is checked numerically for massive electrons and for massless electrons in graphene. The drift velocity at smooth electrostatically defined edges lacks such universality. In the case of anisotropic dispersion, the semiclassical drift velocity at the hard edge reduces to a coordinate-velocity correlation along the cyclotron orbit.

### Paper [153]
Title: Ambient-pressure superconductivity in bulk single crystals of La$_5$Ni$_4$O$_{10}$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12788
Authors: R. Zhang, P. Reiss, A. Krajewska, M. Isobe, H. Takagi, P. Puphal
Abstract: Superconductivity in the Ruddlesden-Popper (RP) nickelates has so far been confined either to multilayer compounds under high pressure or to square-planar infinite-layer-type films stabilized by epitaxial strain. Here we report the high-pressure high-temperature (HPHT) growth of bulk single crystals of the quadruple-layer RP nickelate La$_5$Ni$_4$O$_{13}$ ($n=4$) and reproduce the quintuple-layer La$_6$Ni$_5$O$_{16}$ ($n=5$) synthesis, two members of the series that had previously been accessible only as thin films. Topotactic reduction of La$_5$Ni$_4$O$_{13}$ single crystals with CaH$_2$ yields the square-planar phase La$_5$Ni$_4$O$_{10}$, which we show to be a bulk, ambient-pressure superconductor with $T_\mathrm{c}^\mathrm{onset} \approx 18\,\mathrm{K}$, a shielding fraction above 60\%, and a specific-heat jump that confirms a bulk pairing transition. All crystals show besides superconductivity a coexistence with a density-wave or metal-insulator transition. The upper critical field shows a small anisotropy, $\gamma \approx 2.4$, which remains finite as $T \to T_c$, indicating three-dimensional superconductivity with substantial coupling between the four-layer blocks. These results identify the square-planar nickelates $R_{n+1}$Ni$_n$O$_{2n+2}$ as a bulk platform in which the layer number alone tunes the nickel valence into the superconducting regime, without chemical substitution, pressure, or epitaxial constraint.

### Paper [154]
Title: High Pressure Material Phase Identification with Gaussian Kernel Neural Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12840
Authors: Cameron Ryan, Yasantha Pasindu Hetti Kankanamalage, Shuai Zhang
Abstract: Phase identification is a computational task involving the classification of material structure using the positions of atoms. Using data from Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations, our task is to identify the material phase of a set of atoms, based on how the simulations predict their positioning relative to each other. By leveraging existing research on mechanistic neural network theory, particularly Neural Tangent Kernels (NTK), we develop a new and efficient method for classifying simulated material samples. Our method involves defining a set of "atom detectors" that are given positions around the origin in cartesian coordinates. The sum of Gaussians centered at each atom is calculated at each atom detector, and is used as the input to a neural network. We present theoretical and experimental evidence to show that this method is highly effective and efficient at the task. Our method obtains better or similar accuracy scores to commonly used methods while having a major computational speedup.

### Paper [155]
Title: Enhancing valley splitting in Si/SiGe Wiggle Well quantum dots through integrated stressor-gate architectures
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12850
Authors: E. S. Joseph, Talise Oh, D. E. Savage, D. S. Stone, DeAnna Campbell, Benjamin D. Woods, Mark Friesen, M. A. Eriksson
Abstract: Engineering large and reproducible valley splittings is essential for the scalable operation of silicon spin qubits. In Si/SiGe quantum dot devices, valley splitting arises predominantly from atomic-scale disorder and is therefore inherently random, motivating approaches that introduce a large deterministic contribution. Strain-assisted Wiggle Wells provide a route to deterministic valley splitting by combining an oscillatory Ge concentration in the Si well with shear strain; however, generating the required strain in a manner compatible with large-scale architectures remains a challenge. Here, we propose patterned stressed thin films as a scalable approach for generating shear strain in the quantum well of Si/SiGe quantum dot devices.
