Abstract: Investigating the drivers of superconducting critical temperature trends in cuprates is crucial for uncovering the mechanism of high-temperature superconductivity. Here we study this problem in the canonical model of the copper-oxygen plane, the Emery model, with cellular dynamical mean-field theory. Using the Zaanen-Sawatzky-Allen diagram as a guiding framework, we systematically quantify how the maximum superconducting critical temperature $T_c^{\rm max}$ depends on the copper-oxygen energy distance and on the local repulsion on the copper orbital. The latter is the dominant variable in varying $T_c^{\rm max}$. Unexpectedly, $T_c^{\rm max}$ is optimized not only near the charge-transfer insulator to metal boundary, consistent with previous findings, but also deep in the charge-transfer regime, revealing an unexplored mechanism. Then we link model parameters to physical observables, identifying the charge-transfer gap size and the oxygen hole content as two mechanisms controlling $T_c^{\rm max}$. $T_c^{\rm max}$ increases monotonically as the oxygen hole content increases and the charge gap size decreases. Our work provides predictions for proposed realizations of the Emery model with ultracold atoms and a theoretical framework for understanding key experimental trends in hole-doped cuprates.

### Paper [261]
Title: Bright and Dark Excitons in CrSBr: Local Ligand-Field Character and Band-Coherent Optical Selection Rules
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08355
Authors: Swagata Acharya, Jessica McDivitt, Dimitar Pashov, Mark van Schilfgaarde, Justin C. Johnson, Jeffrey L. Blackburn
Abstract: Magnetic van der Waals semiconductors such as CrSBr host an intricate exciton landscape. Quasiparticle self-consistent GW theory with electron-hole ladder vertex corrections (QSGWc) has established the gap and the character of the bright XA (1.34 eV) and XB (1.8 eV) excitons. Why these bright excitons coexist, within a few tens of meV, with companions orders of magnitude darker has remained unexplained. Here we show that brightness is fixed by a symmetry of the structure: a mirror-glide exchanges the two Cr atoms of each layer, every exciton has a definite parity under it, and bright and dark partners are its even and odd members. The even sector carries the b-axis dipole; the odd sector is suppressed by about three orders of magnitude. The dark partners of XA and XB occupy the same region of k-space as the bright states, and dark states such as the one at 1.50 eV are dipole-forbidden by inversion symmetry. Frenkel and Wannier-Mott labels describe what an exciton is made of, but brightness is decided by symmetry and by the coherent addition of transition dipoles. This bare structure is the reference for magnon, phonon and photon couplings.

### Paper [262]
Title: Phonon Angular Momentum Pumping in Curved Membranes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14868
Authors: Pablo A. Morales
Abstract: Flexural vibrations dominate the low-energy spectrum of a flat crystalline membrane but do not couple to phonon Hall viscosity. We show that static curvature couples normal motion to in-plane strain, allowing an oscillatory flexural drive to generate elliptical in-plane motion and pump phonon angular momentum along the surface normal. A net angular momentum persists in randomly rippled membranes, with its magnitude and handedness controlled by the drive and ripple spectra. Transfer of the pumped angular momentum to the support produces a steady torque that reverses with the magnetic field and scales with the mean-square curvature. Using the measured bulk Hall viscosity of $\alpha$-RuCl$_3$ to set the scale, we estimate a torque of order $10^{-23}$--$10^{-22}\,\mathrm{N\,m}$ for a few-layer membrane, within reach of demonstrated torsional sensors. The same flexural-to-shear response provides a probe of phonon Hall viscosity in atomically thin crystals.

### Paper [263]
Title: Quadrature magnetoresistance scaling reflects linear field dependence rather than strange metallicity of high-temperature superconductors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18685
Authors: D. B. Zhou, Y. Yang, L. F. Feng, M. F. Zhao, Z. Y. Jia, K. H. Gao
Abstract: The quadrature scaling of magnetoresistance has been widely adopted as a hallmark of the strange metal state. However, whether this scaling signals quantum criticality or reflects conventional transport behavior remains controversial. Here, by systematically investigating the magnetotransport properties of NiTe2 nanosheets, we demonstrate that the quadrature scaling is not a unique signature of strange metallicity. We find that the scaling holds only when the crossover field Bc, marking the transition from quadratic to linear magnetoresistance, is sufficiently small relative to the applied field range. Through controlled simulations, we show that the scaling emerges whenever linear magnetoresistance dominates, irrespective of its origin, and fails when the linear regime is inaccessible. This conclusion is supported by observations in SrTiO3 based heterostructures, where quadrature scaling appears despite the absence of strange metal behavior. Our results establish that the quadrature scaling merely reflects the presence of linear magneto resistance, urging caution in using this scaling as a diagnostic tool for exploring the strange metal state.

### Paper [264]
Title: Coupled Magnetic and Lattice Excitations in Multiferroic Ba3HoRu2O9: Insights from Neutron Scattering, Raman Spectroscopy, and Machine-Learned Phonon Calculations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23490
Authors: Ekta Kushwaha, Sayan Ghosh, Gourab Roy, Mohit Kumar, Manh Duc Le, Jhuma Shannigrahi, Som Datta Kaushik, Devashibhai T. Adroja, Tathamay Basu
Abstract: Inelastic neutron scattering (INS) spectra of correlated 4d-4f multiferroic oxides are difficult to interpret because magnetic, crystal-field, and lattice excitations frequently overlap in energy. The hexagonal 6H perovskite Ba3HoRu2O9 provides an ideal platform to investigate this interplay owing to the coexistence of Ru2O9 molecular units and localized Ho3+ moments. To identify the contributions from these different excitations, we combine INS with linear spin-wave calculations, crystal field analysis, Raman spectroscopy, and machine-learned force field (MLFF) phonon calculations. A magnetic excitation below 6.2 meV, observed only in the low-temperature magnetic phase, is reproduced by linear spin-wave theory with intradimer Ru-Ru and Ru-Ho exchange of comparable size and a Ho single-ion anisotropy, and is therefore consistent with a collective spin-wave excitation of the coupled Ru-Ho magnetic network. At higher energies, broad excitations centered near 20, 39, 70, and 90 meV are observed that persist far above the magnetic ordering temperatures. The magnetic part of the 39 meV excitation, isolated by subtracting the Bose-corrected high-temperature data, follows the Ho3+ magnetic form factor. Crystal-field calculations based on the Stevens formalism place a group of strong Ho3+ transitions in this energy range, while Raman spectroscopy and MLFF phonon calculations identify optical phonons with comparable energies. Together, these complementary results show that the broad INS feature near 39 meV arises from overlapping Ho3+ crystal-field excitations and lattice vibrations. These findings establish a microscopic framework for understanding the interplay between spin, crystal-field, and lattice degrees of freedom in this multiferroic 4d-4f compound

### Paper [265]
Title: Fulde-Ferrell-Larkin-Ovchinnikov transition and quantum criticality in polarized Fermi gases
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28335
Authors: Francesco Pirolo, Leonardo Pisani, Pierbiagio Pieri
Abstract: We investigate the zero-temperature transition from the polarized normal phase to the FFLO state in a two-dimensional Fermi gas by means of a diagrammatic t-matrix approach. We first show that the standard non-self-consistent theory produces an unphysical critical polarization because of a severe violation of the Luttinger theorem. Motivated by this observation, we introduce a minimal self-consistent extension that largely restores compliance with the Luttinger theorem while preserving the analytical simplicity of the original formalism. This leads to a physically consistent critical polarization over the whole interaction range. Building on this improved description, we characterize the quantum critical behavior of the FFLO transition through the quasiparticle decay rates, quasiparticle weights, momentum distributions, and the critical dynamics of both fermionic and bosonic degrees of freedom. We further compare the two- and three-dimensional systems, showing that their critical properties can be understood within a unified geometrical picture based on the nesting of the majority and minority Fermi surfaces. Finally, for a three-dimensional lattice, we demonstrate within the Hertz-Millis framework that vertex corrections are irrelevant, thereby placing the FFLO quantum phase transition in the mean-field universality class, in close analogy with the itinerant antiferromagnet transition.

### Paper [266]
Title: Critical Phases of the extended isotropic $XY$ chain with four-spin interaction
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29006
Authors: Nika Kurdadze, Giorgi Gogaberishvili, G. I. Japaridze
Abstract: Using the Jordan-Wigner transformation we calculate exactly the ground state and low-temperature thermodynamic properties of the spin $S=1/2$ isotropic $XX$ chain with four spin interaction. In terms of the equivalent spinless fermion (SF) representation the system is viewed as a lattice fermion gas with nearest-neighbor ($J$) and next-next-next-neighbor ($J^{\ast}/4$) hopping. It is shown that with the increase of four spin coupling, at $J^{\ast}_{c} = 4J/3$ the system experiences the Lifshitz type topological phase transition characterized by the tripling of Fermi points. The quantum phase transition (QPT) point marks transition from a gapless spin-liquid phase of standard $XX$ chain into again a gapless spin-liquid phase with different character of power-low decay of spin correlations. At the transition point the free fermion dispersion relation shows flattering at Fermi points, what determines singular character of density of states $\rho(\omega)\sim (\omega/J)^{-2/3}$ and as a consequence unconventional temperature dependence of heat capacity of the system $C\sim (T/J^{\ast})^{1/3}$, and singular magnetic susceptibility of the system $\chi(H)\sim (H/J^{\ast})^{-2/3}$.
  In the case of alternating magnetic field the system is characterized by the rich ground state phase diagram which contains fully polarized (ferromagnetic), gapped antiferromagnetic (AFM) and spin liquid phases. At the transition point from the gapped AFM phase into the gapless polarized spin liquid phase the system shows rapid increase of magnetization $m\sim(H-H_c)^{1/6}$ and magnetic susceptibility a singular behavior as $\chi(H)\sim (H-H_c)^{-5/6}$.

### Paper [267]
Title: Memory-dependent electronic friction for nonadiabatic dynamics at metal surfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12572
Authors: Xuexun Lu, Connor L. Box, Nils Hertl, Reinhard J. Maurer
Abstract: Electronic excitation induced by nuclear motion is a key energy dissipation channel in chemical dynamics at metal surfaces. Here, nonadiabatic effects can be treated via molecular dynamics with electronic friction, where they act as frictional drag and fluctuation force contributions. Commonly, the Markov approximation is imposed, so memory effects are ignored. A theoretical formalism is presented to evaluate tensorial and configuration-dependent electronic friction memory kernels from first principles. We evaluate friction kernels for Newns--Anderson Hamiltonian models as well as within Kohn--Sham density functional theory and analyse their mathematical properties and configuration dependence. These friction kernels capture memory effects that arise from frequency and configuration dependence and are used to study energy loss in hyperthermal atomic and diatomic scattering studied via the classical path approximation. Memory effects lead to an increase of vibrational and a reduction of translational energy loss in the case of nitric oxide scattering on Au(111), leading to an increase of directional anisotropy of friction. Importantly, memory-dependent evaluation of electronic friction removes the need to define a single effective Markovian friction coefficient from the structured frequency-dependent electronic response.

### Paper [268]
Title: Quasi-periodicity enforces Berry phase discontinuity in crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14489
Authors: Emanuele Maggio
Abstract: The Berry phase of an isolated electronic state is evaluated for different integration paths in reciprocal space, thanks to Riemann-Bloch states providing a consistent description in the whole Brillouin zone.
  The relationship with the bounded position operator is investigated for the open-path Berry phase and with different form factors of the constituent Gaussian type atomic orbitals (GTOs).
  When the Berry phase is evaluated over a closed loop in reciprocal space, a regularisation procedure in the complex plane is adopted and additional \textit{quasi}-symmetries are exploited here for the first time in order to rationalise the occurrence of non-analytical jumps as a function of the winding number or of the GTO broadening.

### Paper [269]
Title: Localized Excitons and Exciton-Phonon Coupling in Antiferromagnetic AgCrP2S6
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18394
Authors: Jessica McDivitt, Dimitar Pashov, Mark van Schilfgaarde, Justin C. Johnson, Jeffrey L. Blackburn, Anna A. Berseneva, Swagata Acharya
Abstract: AgCrP2S6 combines a low-symmetry thiophosphate framework with an antiferromagnetic Cr sublattice and nonmagnetic Ag sites, where covalency and magnetic localization compete in the low-energy optical response. Using single-crystal x-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy, lattice-dynamical calculations, and self-consistent vertex-corrected many-body methods, we determine its structural, electronic, and excitonic properties. The material remains monoclinic from 100 to 300 K, with low-temperature Ag-site disorder, and is dynamically stable. Many-body calculations find a reduced quasiparticle gap relative to Cr trihalides, yet the lowest excitons stay predominantly local: a weakly bright, strongly anisotropic Frenkel exciton near 1.4 eV with dominant onsite d-d character and ligand-assisted d-p admixture. In the C2h setting this state is mainly Bu, with Ag diagonal and Bg bright-dark phonon coupling channels. Stronger p-d hybridization narrows the gap, while antiferromagnetic exchange and Ag dilution suppress intersite coherence. Cation substitution and ligand-ion engineering thus provide a transferable handle for tuning exciton localization in related 2D magnets, with implications for boson coupling and spintronics.

### Paper [270]
Title: Pair size versus coherence length and quantum geometry in the spin-orbit coupled BCS-BEC crossover
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21110
Authors: R. N. Kalkan, M. Iskin
Abstract: We investigate the interplay between pairing correlations and quantum geometry in the spin-orbit-coupled BCS-BEC crossover. Working in the helicity basis, we derive the exact pair-size tensor for the two-body bound states and show that it separates into an intraband contribution and a quantum-geometric interband contribution governed by the quantum metric. We extend this decomposition to two distinct many-body length scales at zero temperature: the Cooper-pair size from mean-field BCS theory and the coherence length from Gaussian fluctuation theory. Although the two almost coincide in the BCS regime, the former measures the internal extent of a pair while the latter measures the long-wavelength response of the order parameter. Evaluating both for three-dimensional Rashba, three-dimensional Weyl, and two-dimensional Rashba spin-orbit couplings, we find that the pair size decreases monotonically with spin-orbit coupling, whereas the coherence length develops a pronounced minimum, associated with the collapse of the noninteracting Fermi surface onto the ring or sphere of helicity-band minima, before growing again as the composite bosons become weakly interacting. The quantum-geometric contributions reach up to about 30\% of the squared pair size and squared coherence length, tracking the same changes in the helicity Fermi-surface topology.

### Paper [271]
Title: Reservoir-Engineered Josephson Diode in a Dark Andreev Channel
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27213
Authors: Jianxiong Zhai, Zelei Zhang, Jiawei Yan
Abstract: We propose a reservoir-engineered Josephson diode in a double-dot junction with symmetric coherent couplings. A relative phase in the coupling to a common loss reservoir selects a rapidly decaying bright orbital and an orthogonal dark Andreev channel. In the quantum Zeno regime, virtual bright-sector transitions set the dark-channel occupation while preserving its coherent supercurrent. For junctions in the infinite-gap Zeno regime, the steady-state current factorizes as $I_{\rm ss}=I_A P_\gamma$: a reciprocal spectral current $I_A$ is reweighted by the reservoir-selected occupation imbalance $P_\gamma$, which generates rectification. The resulting nonreciprocal current remains finite as reservoir leakage decreases with increasing loss strength. Furthermore, local Coulomb repulsion tunes a dark-pair resonance that enables near-ideal rectification, with induced two-particle loss essential to the quantitative description of the steady current. Keldysh-Lindblad calculations confirm that the results remain robust at finite loss strength and superconducting gap.

### Paper [272]
Title: Bound state of a He-3 atom at free He-4 surfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27664
Authors: Massimo Boninsegni
Abstract: Quantum Monte Carlo simulations confirm the existence of a bound state of a single He-3 atom at a free superfluid He-4 surface in three dimensions, localized within a ~10-A thick low-density He-4 surface layer, with a binding energy of approximately 5 K with respect to vacuum. As the temperature is raised above ~ 1 K the He-3 atom leaves the surface to dissolve into the superfluid. The situation is entirely different in two dimensions, as the He-3 atom is excluded from the superfluid and no surface bound state exists. Results are also presented for the a He-3 atom binding to nanoscale size He-4 clusters adsorbed on weak substrates, a physical system that may allow for the observation of some of the physics predicted for extended surfaces. It is shown that a He-3 bound state localized at the perimeter of sufficiently small (a few tens of atoms) clusters exists not just on Cs, as previously reported, but on all alkali substrates.

### Paper [273]
Title: Linker Functionalization and pH Tuning Enhance Solar-Driven Catalytic CO$_2$ Reduction in MOF-5
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02256
Authors: Julia Santana-Andreo, Joshua Edzards, Surender Kumar, Caterina Cocchi
Abstract: The wide band gap of metal-organic framework (MOF) 5 constrains its use in photocatalytic carbon dioxide (CO$_2$) reduction despite its high porosity and favorable mass-transport properties. Adopting a state-of-the-art first-principles approach, we systematically investigate the effects of volumetric strain, metal-node substitution, linker functionalization, and pH control as knobs to improve the CO$_2$ photocatalytic ability of MOF-5. Strain and metal-node substitution negligibly affect the gap, whereas linker functionalization narrows it into the visible range via in-gap states while preserving reduction-side alignment at pH = 0. The resulting reduction energetics are strongly sensitive to both linker functionalization and pH. Halogenated and hydroxylated frameworks provide access primarily to HCOOH, CO, and HCHO under alkaline conditions, principally with the Mg and Zn nodes, whereas COOH functionalization offers the broadest thermodynamic accessibility across the full CO$_2$ reduction sequence. NH$_2$ retains thermodynamic feasibility for all target reduction pathways but with larger overpotentials, while NO$_2$ generally yields unfavorable reduction energetics. Crucially, within the COOH series, the choice of the metal node tunes the fundamental gap by over 1 eV with only minor changes in the reduction overpotentials, placing Sr- and Ba-based architectures as the most favorable ones for broad product selectivity with visible-light excitation. Linker functionalization substantially reduces the spatial overlap of the frontier states, promoting photoinduced charge separation. Taken together, these results establish linker functionalization and solution pH as complementary design levers for independently tuning light absorption and CO$_2$-reduction energetics in MOF-5, establishing a rational and viable route for designing efficient MOF-based photocatalysts.

### Paper [274]
Title: Such stuff as magic is made on: compact operator algebra, stabilizer polytope and the structure of reduced density matrices in a Kitaev spin liquid
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03485
Authors: Snigdh Sabharwal, Nic Shannon, Paul Skrzypczyk
Abstract: Quantum many-body systems exhibit rich and complex behaviour. Magic has recently emerged as a powerful new diagnostic for probing such systems, complementing other key features such as many-body entanglement. Here, starting from an investigation of the onset of magic within subsystems of a larger many-body quantum system, we show that intricate structures emerge which shed important light on the underlying many-body physics. Focusing on the Kitaev honeycomb model, we first identify the temperature below which local subsystems acquire magic. Remarkably, we find that the optimal magic witnesses active at the onset of magic reveal compact operator spaces that continue to capture the local thermal states throughout their subsequent evolution. For the six-site hexagonal marginal, this connection can be made stronger: the same operator space emerges independently from the symmetries of the local marginal and forms the symmetry-resolved plaquette algebra. When these symmetries are realized exactly, the local state lies entirely within this algebra and the reduced robustness of magic is equal to the full robustness of magic. The same reduced description can also be applied to other local quantum resources, which we illustrate using genuine multipartite entanglement. Furthermore, the operator spaces possess a rich algebraic structure in the form of finite-dimensional Euclidean Jordan algebras, with a natural interpretation in terms of bond and bond-cycle operators. Our general methodology therefore show how the onset of local magic can reveal a compact, physically meaningful operator structure underlying the finite-temperature Kitaev spin liquid, and opens up a new avenue towards reduced descriptions of quantum resources in many-body systems.

### Paper [275]
Title: On Rare Nonresonant Regions and Subdiffusive Transport in an Interacting Disordered Quantum Chain
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13834
Authors: A. Scardicchio, S. L. Sondhi
Abstract: We study rare nonresonant regions in a canonical one-dimensional disordered quantum spin chain by directly implementing the iterated Schrieffer-Wolff construction underlying the recent work of De Roeck, Giacomin, Huveneers and Prosniak on subdiffusive transport. For finite systems, we compute the probability that a disorder realization remains nonresonant through successive scales of the flow, while separately monitoring operator proliferation and the spatial localization of dressed local observables. The survival probability decays at most exponentially with system size over the numerically accessible regime. Resolving this decay scale by scale yields failure rates associated with successive Schrieffer-Wolff steps. The first rate is obtained analytically in excellent agreement with numerics, while higher-scale rates decrease rapidly throughout the regime studied. These results support the summability mechanism required for exponentially rare but parametrically long nonresonant regions, which provide the insulating bottlenecks responsible for subdiffusive transport. At the smallest couplings studied, the survival probability under our conditions exceeds the rigorous lower bound by six to seven orders of magnitude, demonstrating how conservative the constants required by the proof are while confirming that its underlying physical mechanism is quantitatively visible at accessible scales. More broadly, our results show how direct numerical implementations can provide an independent and physically transparent test of technically demanding constructive proofs, a methodology that may become increasingly useful as machine-assisted proofs become more common.

### Paper [276]
Title: Multiple Andreev Reflection Spectroscopy of Spin-Resolved Proximity States
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19380
Authors: A. A. Golubov, M. Yu. Kupriyanov
Abstract: We study coherent multiple Andreev reflections (MAR) in short constrictions connecting superconductor-ferromagnet bilayers with parallel exchange fields. Proximity-induced spectral reconstruction produces split and redistributed differential-resistance anomalies, modifying both odd- and even-order subharmonic features, unlike a rigid Zeeman shift of BCS spectra. The shifted anomalies are approximately governed by the interval between the parent superconducting gap edge and an exchange-dependent spectral singularity. Qualitative spectral-interval estimates for antiparallel alignment are given in the Supplemental Material. Channel-transparency averaging extends the description from short ballistic constrictions to short diffusive multichannel junctions, where the characteristic anomalies survive. These findings provide a route to probing spin-resolved proximity states through two-terminal charge transport.

### Paper [277]
Title: Entropy-Driven Initiation and Cytoskeletal Viscoelasticity in Endocytosis: An Onsager Variational Framework
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26734
Authors: Jinjie Liu, Zhongcan Ouyang, Hao Wu
Abstract: Receptor-mediated endocytosis requires a particle to approach the cell membrane to within a few nanometers before ligand--receptor binding can occur. Existing continuum models often start from an already established contact and do not explicitly describe how crowding particles on the extracellular side influence the distribution of the particle near the membrane. We examine entropic depletion forces as one possible nonspecific contribution to this initial approach. For ideal depletants, the Asakura--Oosawa excluded-volume construction gives an exact depletion potential for the planar geometry before contact. The potential and force vanish continuously at the onset of excluded-volume overlap. This interaction provides a possible contribution to membrane proximity before specific binding, while its extension to curved wrapping geometries requires additional approximation. Within a reduced continuum model, we combine depletion attraction, ligand--receptor binding, membrane deformation, and cytoskeletal viscoelastic dissipation. The viscoelastic contact is formulated through a hereditary integral and a standard linear solid. The kinetic model gives a conditional minimum ligand density for complete engulfment, a finite particle-size window, and a stiffness-dependent upper limit. When the stationary radius lies inside the domain of finite positive wrapping times, the estimated wrapping time has a minimum at a radius that decreases with increasing binding energy density. At fixed viscosity and other independent parameters, the same time approximation predicts slower wrapping as cell stiffness increases. The two positive roots defining the size window merge at a limiting parameter value, which characterizes closure of the admissible size interval. Depletion attraction is interpreted as one possible contribution to particle-membrane association, alongside electrostatic interactions

### Paper [278]
Title: An information circuit driven by a skyrmion Maxwell's demon
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36955
Authors: Soma Miki, Yoshishige Suzuki, Ryo Ishikawa, Yuki Hibino, Shunya Morishita, Hiroto Imanishi, Keigo Adachi, Yoichi Shiota, Takayuki Nozaki, Minori Goto, Hikaru Nomura, Shigemi Mizukami, Eiiti Tamura
Abstract: At the nanoscale, observation and feedback may transform thermal fluctuations into valuable resources. Nevertheless, a framework for experimentation with the resulting probability currents has yet to be firmly established. Here, we present an information heat engine incorporating two interacting magnetic skyrmions in adjacent potential wells. Feedback from a skyrmion in Brownian motion at room temperature to the other yields a Maxwell's demon and a circulating probability current in their joint state space. A linear relationship is confirmed between the probability current and the information affinity, the thermodynamic conjugate value, within certain response range and conditions. We identify the slope as the "information conductance" analogous to electrical conductance. The information conductance is modified by skyrmion interactions and scattering, while Kirchhoff's laws remain valid. These results establish a circuit representation of the state space of an information heat engine with potential applications to computing devices, biological systems and chemical reactions.

### Paper [279]
Title: Piecewise Parabolic Kinetic Energies and Classical Time Crystals
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39201
Authors: Stavros Theodorakis, Athina Hadjizorzi
Abstract: We present explicit, exact analytic solutions for classical time crystals embedded in a parabolic potential for the position. While Lagrangians with a quartic kinetic energy yield complex trajectories governed by inscrutable elliptic integrals, we demonstrate that unconventional piecewise parabolic kinetic energies -minimized at nonzero values of the speed- admit simple, transparent periodic solutions. In these systems, the speed acquires definite periodic discontinuities but remains always nonzero. The requirement that the action be the smallest available determines the branch that the solution will follow, ensuring in addition that it is stable there and rendering the Hamiltonian single-valued as a function of momentum. Furthermore, we introduce a powerful piecewise parabolic emulation framework to approximate highly non-linear kinetic energies, demonstrating its utility on both quartic time crystals and the relativistic harmonic oscillator. We show that finding exact solutions to these emulated models provides accurate, physically transparent simulacra of complicated problems, offering a robust alternative to standard numerical approximations for non-quadratic kinetic energies.

### Paper [280]
Title: Bogoliubov excitations of a Bose-Einstein condensate in the presence of the non-Hermitian skin effect
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16525
Authors: Carlo Panu, Ulf Peschel, Oleg A. Egorov, Elena A. Ostrovskaya, Eliezer Estrecho
Abstract: The non-Hermitian skin effect is one of the most remarkable topological features of non-conservative physics. It causes a macroscopic accumulation of states at the boundaries of a confined system, opening new ways for controlling and directing waves in non-Hermitian settings. The non-Hermitian skin effect has recently been observed in experiments with Bose-Einstein condensates of exciton polaritons and ultracold bosonic quantum gases. Here, we develop a non-Hermitian extension of the Bogoliubov-de-Gennes theory for the collective excitations of the condensate, both in the semiclassical (mean-field) and second-quantised framework. By considering a trapped condensate under the action of an imaginary gauge field, we find that while the repulsive interactions oppose the localisation of the condensate, its excitations continue to display a strong skin effect. As a consequence, in the presence of harmonic confinement, we predict a strong shift in the dipole oscillation frequency, in contrast to Hermitian systems, where this frequency is fixed by the trap dimensions. Furthermore, we find that a second-quantised treatment removes the shift of the dipole oscillation frequency entirely. The two approaches describe different physical regimes, and identifying which one a given platform realises requires a microscopic open-system description. We suggest that this discrepancy between the two theoretical approaches can be resolved in experiments.

### Paper [281]
Title: Universal Scaling and Geometric Correction of High-Frequency Mass Transfer in Porous Electrodes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20211
Authors: Md Abdul Hamid, Kyle C. Smith
Abstract: Classical film theory predicts a finite diffusion-limited current at porous electrodes. Transient operation can nevertheless produce peak interfacial fluxes above this steady limit, although the time-averaged flux remains bounded. Here, we use a frequency-domain volume-averaging framework to study this transient response in porous media. In the perfectly reactive limit, the spectral Sherwood number approaches $\Shnd\sim\sqrt{\iu\wstar}$ at high frequency, with magnitude proportional to $\omega^{1/2}$, phase $\pi/4$, and a universal leading prefactor. A boundary-layer analysis shows that this response follows from local transient diffusion and is independent of the global pore geometry. Numerical results for parallel plates, circular tubes, and twelve porous microstructures confirm the scaling across variations in particle shape, symmetry, porosity, array arrangement, flow orientation, and flow strength. We also derive the first geometry-dependent correction, which depends on interfacial area, surface curvature, and sharp edges, and verify it without fitted parameters. Comparisons with published impedance measurements for a carbon-felt fiber and a vanadium flow-battery electrode are consistent with the predicted transport response. Finally, we derive bounds on pulse duration and identify operating windows for representative redox couples. These results establish a temporal route to enhanced peak transport in porous electrodes without requiring surface charge, confinement, or electrokinetic instability.

### Paper [282]
Title: The Extended Diode Equation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32090
Authors: Jianhong Yang, Gonghao Sun, Tinglan Li
Abstract: For more than seven decades, the p-n junction current-voltage characteristic has been derived by treating majority carriers as an inert background and conflating the junction voltage with the applied bias. Here we show that the base electric field, generated by the coupling of excess majority and minority carriers, fundamentally alters the internal potential distribution. This self-consistent framework yields a closed-form extended diode equation that reproduces the high-injection roll-off without invoking an external series resistance, thereby eliminating an empirical patch that has persisted in the classical theory.

### Paper [283]
Title: A Self-Consistent Two-Carrier Drift-Diffusion Framework for p-n Junctions: Extended Diode Equation, Power Dissipation, and Base Transit Time
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32112
Authors: Jianhong Yang, Tinglan Li, Gonghao Sun
Abstract: The classical theory of p-n junctions derives the current-voltage characteristic from minority-carrier diffusion alone: majority carriers are treated as an inert background, the quasi-neutral base as field-free, and the junction voltage as equal to the applied bias. These premises fail together under high bias and high-level injection, where the observed current roll-off is reproduced only by appending an empirical series resistance outside the junction physics. Here we develop a self-consistent two-carrier drift-diffusion framework that introduces no assumption beyond the classical ones. Excess majority carriers are shown to be electrostatically forced transport participants: the ambipolar diffusion equation, closed by exact two-sided boundary conditions, yields a unified excess-carrier profile. The resulting base field separates into an ohmic and a Dember component, and all four current components follow in closed form, their sum being position independent. At device level the framework closes into an extended diode equation that contains the Shockley equation as its low-injection, long-base limit and reproduces the n=2 roll-off intrinsically. The junction voltage emerges as a self-limiting drop confined to the space-charge region and separated from the applied bias by exact base drops. Two device-level corrections are quantified: at high current, 75-90% of the dissipation migrates from the junction into the quasi-neutral base, and field-assisted acceleration shortens the base transit time by up to a factor of six. Three parameter-free predictions follow: a universal ideality-factor crossover that passes through 3/2 when the boundary excess equals the doping, a high-injection onset voltage that scales as 119 mV per decade of doping, and a geometry factor that survives into high injection.

### Paper [284]
Title: Exciton-driven coherent dressing in transient absorption
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36263
Authors: Jinyuan Wu, Chang Shu, Yang-hao Chan, Diana Y. Qiu
Abstract: While coherent excitons have recently been observed to dress electronic states in time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (tr-ARPES), exciton-driven dressing in optical spectroscopy remains largely unexplored. This work develops a theoretical and computational approach to exciton-driven dressing effects in transient absorption spectroscopy in the time-dependent adiabatic GW formalism and derives two symmetry- and band topology-protected selections rules for exciton-dressed transient absorption, unique from tr-ARPES. We then numerically calculate exciton-dressed transient absorption spectra of monolayer MoS2 and demonstrate the selection rules and their lifting after symmetry breaking.

### Paper [285]
Title: Light-Cone Friedel Oscillations: a Window into van Hove Superconductivity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40648
Authors: Archisman Panigrahi, Vladislav Poliakov, Leonid Levitov
Abstract: Van Hove (vH) singularities can enhance superconductivity by generating a large pairing susceptibility. Yet, it remains challenging to experimentally establish whether pairing really occurs near these points. We show that the saddle-point dispersion near a vH point gives rise to a `light-cone' vH-selective behavior of Friedel oscillations, which is drastically different from the familiar concentric circles. Each vH point maps onto its own real-space light-cone sector, allowing STM around a single impurity to measure whether---and how strongly---that saddle-point quasiparticle sector is gapped. A superconducting gap near vH points suppresses the oscillations in the directions corresponding to peaks in the gap function angular dependence, a behavior that provides a signature of vH-related pairing at STM-accessible length scales. This STM protocol, targeting vH superconductivity, is capable of determining whether superconducting pairing emerges near the vH points or elsewhere on the Fermi surface.

### Paper [286]
Title: Variational wavefunctions for ideal anyon gas: superfluid and skyrmion Hall crystal
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40657
Authors: Ya-Hui Zhang
Abstract: The ground state of an ideal gas of anyons with statistical angle $\theta\in(0,\pi)$ is a long-standing open problem that has gained renewed relevance with the observation of fractional Chern insulators. We construct variational wavefunctions on the sphere that incorporate the exact anyon statistics, using composite-boson, composite-fermion and parton states as starting points for neural-network optimization. For both spinless and SU(2) semions, independent constructions support a paired anyon superfluid whose energy lies well below that of the best crystal states. Liquids and crystals compete more closely for spinless anyons at $\theta=\pi/3$. For $\theta=\pi/3$ anyons with SU(3) flavor, we identify two competing candidates: a skyrmion Hall crystal, whose pinned flavor texture gives an integer Hall response of the anyons, and a chiral color superconductor of six-anyon clusters. This competition offers a possible explanation for the adjacent reentrant integer quantum anomalous Hall and superconducting phases in twisted MoTe$_2$. In this setting, the color superconductor has $f+if$ symmetry but differs from its BCS counterpart by 3 in chiral central charge. We also provide parameter-free analytical wavefunctions for the color-superconductor ansatz, which reach energies comparable to, or even lower than, those of the best optimized states for SU(3) anyons at $\theta=\pi/3$ and for SU(2) semions.

### Paper [287]
Title: Mechanical modulation of the Gutenberg-Richter b-value in hierarchical fault networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02541
Authors: Bin Chen, Mingze Zhao, Puyu Cao
Abstract: The Gutenberg-Richter (G-R) law is seismology's most robust empirical regularity, yet a unified mechanical explanation for its slope-the b-value-remains a subject of ongoing debate. Here, we propose a framework suggesting that the b-value is mechanically modulated by the hierarchical architecture of fault networks. By coupling fracture mechanics with hierarchical network topology, we derive a generalized closed-form expression: b=\frac{3}{3+2\gamma}\frac{\log_{10}k}{\log_{10}q}. We further link these topological parameters to rate-and-state friction theory, illustrating that k and q can be mechanistically constrained by the competition between elastic stiffness, frictional healing, and energy dissipation. The empirical universality of b=1 may arise as the system balances these competing mechanisms, tending to reside near a mechanically balanced state (k=q). Furthermore, the dynamic decoupling of k and q under tectonic loading drives the system away from this critical state, providing a mechanical explanation for observed b-value anomalies. The stress-drop scaling exponent {\gamma} enters as a modulated perturbation about the constant-stress ({\gamma}=0) zeroth-order baseline: it shifts b only where source-scaling data independently constrain {\gamma} away from zero. By establishing a mechanistic connection between macroscopic statistical variations and microscopic mechanical processes, this framework may provide a new perspective for interpreting fault dynamics.

### Paper [288]
Title: Anion Engineering Enables Concurrent Optimization of Phonon and Carrier Transport in AMg$_2$X$_2$ Zintl Thermoelectrics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02961
Authors: Ning Wang, Jincheng Yue, Hui Zhang, Xingchen Shen, Wenling Ren, Shuyao Lin, Yanhui Liu, Yue-Wen Fang, Tian Cui
Abstract: The simultaneous suppression of lattice heat transport and enhancement of carrier transport is central to the development of high-performance thermoelectric materials. Here, we investigate the microscopic mechanisms governing phonon and carrier transport in CaAl$_2$Si$_2$-type AMg$_2$X$_2$ (A = Ca, Sr, Ba; X = P, As, Sb, Bi) Zintl compounds using first-principles calculations, machine-learning interatomic potentials, and Boltzmann transport theory. As X changes from P to Bi, the widening phonon band gap restricts three-phonon scattering channels and increases the relative importance of four-phonon processes. In CaMg$_2$Bi$_2$ and BaMg$_2$Bi$_2$, four-phonon scattering dominates the relaxation of low-frequency heat-carrying modes at 900 K. The Bi-based series also exhibits an anomalous increase in lattice thermal conductivity from Ca to Ba despite the increasing cation mass. Mode-resolved analysis links the lower thermal conductivity of CaMg$_2$Bi$_2$ to reduced low-frequency group velocities and stronger anharmonic scattering, with Ca-related mid-frequency vibrations providing additional four-phonon scattering channels. Including both particle-like and coherent contributions, its lattice thermal conductivity reaches 0.23 W$\cdot$m$^{-1}$K$^{-1}$ at 900 K. Meanwhile, the increasing covalent character of the vertical Mg-X bonds is accompanied by enhanced band-edge dispersion and reduced carrier effective masses. These electronic changes, together with conduction-band convergence, favor n-type transport and yield a predicted peak $ZT$ of 3.68 at 900 K in CaMg$_2$Bi$_2$. These results elucidate the interplay of phonon spectral structure and higher-order anharmonicity in thermal transport, alongside the distinct electronic response to anion substitution.

### Paper [289]
Title: Bayesian-optimized magnetic-field-gradient sensing with a spin-orbit-coupled Bose-Einstein condensate
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04719
Authors: Shixiang Chen, Juan Wang, Yan Li
Abstract: Building on our previous study of the expansion dynamics of a spin-orbit-coupled Bose-Einstein condensate, we propose a scheme for magnetic-field-gradient sensing that relates the maximum rotational deflection angle of the spin-density distribution to the magnetic-field gradient. We analyze the dependence of the sensing performance on key parameters and perform a systematic parameter optimization. A Bayesian optimization algorithm is introduced to efficiently identify the optimal parameter combination, thereby enhancing the sensitivity of magnetic-field-gradient sensing. Numerical simulations under the adopted noise model show that, compared with the parameter set used in our previous work, the optimized gradiometer improves the sensitivity by a factor of five. This work provides a new route for magnetic-field-gradient sensing and offers a reference for machine-learning-assisted quantum precision measurement.

### Paper [290]
Title: Ta-based S-I-S junctions for integrated superconducting electronics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04982
Authors: Trent Borman, Cameron Bullock, Cole Chason, Bradley Christensen, Jonathan Cochran, Emad Din, Evan Donohue, Jason Flagg, A. L. Graninger, Eric Jones, Robert Klaes, Shawn Keebaugh, Erin Kennedy, John Kula, Jason Lapano, Melissa Loving, Christopher Pasco, Devika Pathak, Tam Pham, Timothy Pillsbury, Robert Pownall, Paige Quarterman, Jacob Ruf, Jade Simmons, Nathan Siwak, Joel Strand, Yong Mo Yang
Abstract: In this work, we have presented S-I-S Josephson junctions with $\rm\alpha$-Ta electrodes and a ${\rm TaO}_x$ barrier. We grew base and top ${\alpha}$-Ta electrodes using seed layer engineering, showing both Nb and ${\rm TaN}_x$ are viable seed layer choices, and particularly emphasize that at room temperature a re-seed layer is required to nucleate $\rm\alpha$-Ta for the top electrode. However, we note that the choice of seed layer can have subtle implications on the electrical tunneling behavior of the junctions. We used room-temperature probing of variable junction areas, combined with Ambegaokar-Baratoff physics, to characterize the wafer scale critical current densities. For 0.4 $\mu$m$^2$ junctions, we found junction spreads as low as 2.0% across the 20 x 20 mm chip relevant for device performance. The entire process was carried out using standard industry foundry processing using 150 mm wafer tooling, which is an important step towards proving that Ta-based junctions can be implemented at scale. We have successfully fabricated and tested junctions with $\rm\alpha$-Ta electrodes and a ${\rm TaO}_x$ insulating tunnel barrier, demonstrating that this is a material platform which merits further investigation and studies for potential application in classical and quantum superconducting technologies.

### Paper [291]
Title: DefectSkills: Guiding AI agents through first-principles calculations for point defects in semiconductors and insulators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05654
Authors: Zhenkun Yuan, Yizhi Zhu, Geoffroy Hautier
Abstract: First-principles defect calculations can now be performed autonomously by AI agents, enabled by established computational methods and open-source Python toolkits. However, reliable and repeatable defect calculations require clear scientific and technical guidance that helps agents resolve ambiguities and avoid errors in calculation setup, execution, postprocessing, and analysis. Here, we present DefectSkills, a set of structured prompts that provides human-in-the-loop guidance to AI agents performing first-principles calculations of point defects in semiconductors and insulators using the Vienna Ab initio Simulation Package (VASP). The prompts encode human expertise and guide agents through unit-cell relaxation, electronic-structure and dielectric calculations, phase-stability and chemical-potential analysis, charged-defect supercell generation and calculations, finite-size corrections, formation-energy calculations and thermodynamic analysis, and data backup. By packaging this expertise as reusable agent skills, DefectSkills supports consistent workflow execution across agentic AI systems and reproducible defect calculations.

### Paper [292]
Title: Kerr-athermal baths enhance cooling in quantum refrigerators
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.10499
Authors: Tanaya Ray, Sayan Mondal, Aparajita Bhattacharyya, Ahana Ghoshal, Debraj Rakshit, Ujjwal Sen
Abstract: We study a three-qubit quantum refrigerator, with a three-body interaction enabling cooling of the target qubit, coupled to harmonic oscillator baths prepared in stationary Kerr-athermal states. The reservoir occupation probabilities are specified by a Kerr-type preparation Hamiltonian, while the bath dynamics during operation are harmonic. We show that these reservoirs lower the steady-state temperature of the cold qubit relative to the thermal-bath reference. The improvement of steady-state cooling is distinct from that of the minimum transient temperature, which is not universally enhanced. In a parameter regime where the thermal reference exhibits only transient cold-qubit cooling, the engineered Kerr-athermal baths can produce a steady-state temperature below the initial value. We also investigate the dependence of the steady-state temperature and minimum attainable temperatures on the anharmonicity in the bath state. Analysis of bath-currents reveals lower steady-state temperatures with nearly unchanged coefficients of performance, revealing that reservoir engineering leads to cold-qubit cooling.

### Paper [293]
Title: Self-correcting qubit in a strongly driven LC resonator
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.03650
Authors: Max Geier, Frederik Nathan
Abstract: We propose a device to realize quantum error correction by dissipation. The device generates Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) states as steady states in a single driven LC resonator. The resonator should have impedance above $h/2e^2\approx 12.9\,{\rm k}\Omega$, and should be driven across a Josephson junction with a high-amplitude voltage tone at frequency near-commensurate with the resonator frequency. In this regime, we show that GKP states emerge as steady-states of the resonator; these states encode a qubit protected against phase-space local noise. Low-lying excitations preserve the quantum information and increase the energy proportionally to a measure of the error degree. Thus, quantum error correction of both bit and phase errors can be achieved with dissipative cooling. Our calculations indicate an achievable coherence time of $ 1000 \times$ the resonator photon lifetime at operating temperatures above $100 \, {\rm mK}$. Beyond steady-state quantum error correction, the device supports protected single-qubit Clifford gates and readout with exponential accuracy corrected by the same dissipative mechanism, as well as magic-state preparation above the distillation threshold.

### Paper [294]
Title: Challenges in Non-Polymeric Crystal Structure Prediction: Why a Geometric, Permutation-Invariant Loss is Needed
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.00832
Authors: Emmanuel Jehanno, Romain Menegaux, Julien Mairal, Sergei Grudinin
Abstract: Crystalline structure prediction is an essential prerequisite for designing materials with targeted properties. Yet, it is still an open challenge in materials design and drug discovery. Despite recent advances in computational materials science, accurately predicting three-dimensional non-polymeric crystal structures remains elusive. In this work, we focus on the molecular assembly problem, where a set~$\mathcal{S}$ of identical rigid molecules is packed to form a crystalline structure. Such a simplified formulation provides a useful approximation to the actual problem. However, while recent state-of-the-art methods have increasingly adopted sophisticated techniques, the underlying learning objective remains ill-posed. We propose a better formulation that introduces a loss function capturing key geometric molecular properties while ensuring permutation invariance over $\mathcal{S}$. Remarkably, we demonstrate that within this framework, a simple regression model already outperforms prior approaches, including flow matching techniques, on the COD-Cluster17 benchmark, a curated non-polymeric subset of the Crystallography Open Database (COD). Our code is available at https://github.com/inria-thoth/SinkFast.

### Paper [295]
Title: Light in Slices: How to Enable Precise Laser Processing?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22665
Authors: Stefan Nolte, J\"orn Bonse, Nadezhda M. Bulgakova
Abstract: Ultrashort (femtosecond, fs) laser pulses have fascinating properties as they allow to confine optical energy on extreme scales in space and time. Such fs-laser pulsed beams can be seen as spatially thin slices of intense light that are radially and axially constrained to the micrometer scale, while simultaneously propagating at the extremely high speed of light. Their high peak intensities and their short time lapse makes such laser pulses unique tools for materials processing, as their duration is shorter than the time required to transfer absorbed optical energy, via electron-phonon coupling, from the electronic system of the solid to its lattice. Hence, the laser pulse energy remains localized during the interaction and does not spread via diffusion into the area surrounding the irradiated region. As one consequence, the fs-laser thus offers increased precision for material modification or ablation accompanied by a reduced heat-affected zone of only a few hundred nanometers. On the other hand, the high laser peak intensities can enable nonlinear material interactions that are rendering unique material excitation and relaxation pathways possible. In this chapter, we briefly review the reasons for the enormous success of ultrashort pulse lasers in materials processing - both for the processing of the surface or in the bulk of solids. We identify the underlying fundamental processes that can limit the precision or the up-scaling of the laser processing towards large volumes, areas, or processing rates. Strategies to overcome such limitations will be outlined and questions on the ultimate limits of laser material processing will be answered.

### Paper [296]
Title: Ultrafast Meissner-Levitated Microrotor
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.24964
Authors: Joel K Jose, Andrea Marchese, Marion Cromb, Hendrik Ulbricht, Andrejs Cebers, Ping Koy Lam, Tao Wang, Andrea Vinante
Abstract: Levitated mechanical systems offer a unique and flexible platform for the realization of ultralow noise sensors and tests of fundamental physics. Here, we report the demonstration of a levitated microrotor, consisting of a magnetic microsphere levitated by the Meissner effect at 4.2 K. The rotor reaches rotation frequencies above 2.3 MHz (138 million rpm) while exhibiting an exceptionally low damping rate 4.75 $\times$10$^{-7}$ Hz. Exploiting the linear dependence of friction on the gas density, we demonstrate high-accuracy absolute pressure sensing over a wide range at cryogenic temperature. Finally, we detect the thermal torque noise at intermediate pressure by operating the rotor in synchronized mode, resulting in a first preliminary narrowband search for axion-like dark matter. More broadly, the unique combination of high rotational speed, low temperature, and low thermal torque noise establishes a new regime for precision measurements, with direct implications for the detection of extremely weak forces and fields.

### Paper [297]
Title: Real-time dynamics with bead-Fourier path integrals. II. Bead-Fourier RPMD
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09961
Authors: Nathan London, Mohammad R. Momeni
Abstract: Feynman path integrals (PIs) have found many uses in approximate quantum dynamics methods for efficiently calculating real-time quantum correlation functions. The PIs typically take the form of discrete imaginary-time slices along a closed path, where the slices form the ``beads'' of a ring polymer (RP) necklace. Some methods, such as centroid molecular dynamics (CMD), use the RP to generate an effective potential for the dynamics, while others, like RP molecular dynamics (RPMD), directly utilize the RP in real-time dynamics in order to incorporate approximate quantum effects. The standard, discretized bead forms of CMD and RPMD may require a large number of RP beads to obtain accurate results for systems with strong quantum effects and at low temperatures. In Paper I (J. Chem. Phys. 2025, 163, 144123), we introduced the bead-Fourier (BF)-CMD method, where we utilized the inclusion of a Fourier sine series to reduce the number of beads needed to converge the CMD effective potential up to eightfold. In this work, we extend RPMD to incorporate BF-PIs in the form of BF-RPMD. We study several implementations of the BF-RPMD method through the calculation of correlation functions for both linear and nonlinear operators. We found that the effectiveness of this method is sensitive to both the type of system and the form of the operators being studied. Our results show that this method is able to produce results on par with standard RPMD, with at worst a twofold and up to an eightfold reduction in the number of beads by including two to three Fourier components.

### Paper [298]
Title: Dynamical entanglement percolation with spatially correlated disorder
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05925
Authors: Lorenzo Cirigliano, Valentina Brosco, Claudio Castellano, Simone Felicetti, Laura Pilozzi, Bernard van Heck
Abstract: The distribution of entanglement between the nodes of a quantum network plays a fundamental role in quantum information applications. In this work, we investigate the dynamics of a network of qubits where each edge corresponds to an independent two-qubit interaction. By applying tools from percolation theory, we study how long-range entanglement, obtained via singlet conversion and swapping from dynamically generated pairs, emerges across the network. We show that the interplay between unitary evolution and spatially correlated disorder leads to a non-standard percolation phenomenology, significantly richer than uniform bond percolation and featuring multi-valued hysteresis-like behaviour. A two-colour correlated bond percolation model, whose phase diagram is determined via numerical simulations and a mean-field theory, fully elucidates the physics behind this phenomenon.

### Paper [299]
Title: Fractional Quantum Hall States: Infinite Matrix Product Representation and its Implications
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12165
Authors: Severin Schraven, Simone Warzel
Abstract: We present a novel matrix product representation of the Laughlin and related fractional quantum Hall wavefunctions based on a rigorous version of the correlators of a chiral quantum field theory. This representation enables the quantitative control of the coefficients of the Laughlin wavefunction times an arbitrary monomial symmetric polynomial when expanded in a Slater determinant or permanent basis. It renders the properties, such as factorization and the renewal structure, inherent in such fractional quantum Hall wavefunctions transparent. We prove bounds on the correlators of the chiral quantum field theory and utilize this representation to demonstrate the exponential decay of connected correlations and a gap in the entanglement spectrum on a thin cylinder.

### Paper [300]
Title: Computationally sufficient statistics for Ising models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.12449
Authors: Abhijith Jayakumar, Shreya Shukla, Marc Vuffray, Andrey Y. Lokhov, Sidhant Misra
Abstract: Learning Gibbs distributions using only sufficient statistics has long been recognized as a computationally hard problem. On the other hand, computationally efficient algorithms for learning Gibbs distributions rely on access to full sample configurations generated from the model. For many systems of interest that arise in physical contexts, expecting a full sample to be observed is not practical, and hence it is important to look for computationally efficient methods that solve the learning problem with access to only a limited set of statistics. We examine the trade-offs between the power of computation and observation within this scenario, employing the Ising model as a paradigmatic example. We show that the couplings of the model can be learned to a desired accuracy from statistics whose order grows linearly with the $\ell_1$ width of the model and only logarithmically with the inverse of the accuracy. In particular, for a fixed accuracy, the required order is proportional to the width. This allows us to infer the model's structure, using statistics whose order grows logarithmically with the inverse of the weakest coupling, and also to learn its magnetic fields. When the width and the accuracy are fixed, the running time is polynomial in the number of variables. We also discuss a setting where prior information about structure of the model is available and show that the learning problem can be solved efficiently with even more limited observational power.

### Paper [301]
Title: g4chargeit: Geant4-based kinetic Monte Carlo simulations of charging in dielectric materials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17332
Authors: Kush P. Gandhi, Advik D. Vira, William M. Farrell, Nikolai Simonov, Alvaro Romero-Calvo, Thomas M. Orlando, Phillip N. First, Zhigang Jiang
Abstract: We present g4chargeit, a kinetic Monte Carlo framework built on Geant4 for self-consistent simulation of time-dependent electrostatic charging in dielectric materials. The model explicitly incorporates stochastic particle transport and scattering processes using validated Geant4 cross-sections, while self-consistently evolving the electric potential and field. As a representative application, we simulate the charging of regolith grains under average dayside conditions on the Moon. The surface of the Moon, in addition to other airless planetary bodies, are regularly exposed to solar ultraviolet photons and solar-wind plasma, creating a radiation environment in which electrostatic interactions among regolith grains become significant. Until now, simulations of regolith charging have often relied on analytical approximations that oversimplify grain geometry and interaction mechanisms. Our Geant4-based simulations reveal charge accumulation within intergrain micro-cavities, leading to repulsive electrostatic forces consistent with experimental observations. The framework establishes a multiscale approach that links microscopic scattering events to the continuity equation of surface charge density and to the formation of macroscopic surface charge patches in complex grain geometries. Although demonstrated here for planetary regolith, the method is general and applicable to a broad range of dielectric charging problems. The code is openly available at https://github.com/kgandhi63/g4chargeit.git.

### Paper [302]
Title: Scaling signatures of near-critical dynamics in large-scale human resting-state electrophysiology
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17820
Authors: Irem Topal, Anna Poggialini, Marco Dal Maschio, Daniele De Martino, Oren Shriki, Fabrizio Lombardi
Abstract: From 1/f noise to neuronal avalanches, evidence of scaling in brain activity has been increasingly linked to tuning to or near criticality. The concept of scaling is intimately related to the renormalization group (RG), in essence providing coarse-grained, simplified descriptions that generalize to classes of diverse physical systems. Following the RG idea, scaling laws have been reported in populations of spiking neurons at microscopic scales. Whether similar scaling principles govern large-scale neural activity in the human brain and how they relate to underlying neural physiology remains unresolved. Here, we analyze large-scale electrophysiological recordings (MEG) of human resting-state brain activity and apply a RG-inspired coarse-graining approach to track collective neural dynamics across spatial scales. We find that multiple observables exhibit robust scale-invariant behavior under coarse-graining: activity variance and correlations grow according to power laws, covariance eigenspectra follow a characteristic scaling relation, and neuronal avalanche statistics and scaling relationships are conserved. Using an analytically tractable neural network model with parameters inferred from data, we show that the observed scaling signatures arise close to, but slightly below, criticality, and that the scaling exponents can be modulated by network inhibitory drives. These findings demonstrate that RG-inspired scaling analysis can uncover signatures of critical dynamics in non-invasive human electrophysiology and suggest a principled route toward estimating excitation-inhibition balance from large-scale brain recordings.

### Paper [303]
Title: Generalized $\mathbb{Z}_p$ toric codes as qudit low-density parity-check codes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20158
Authors: Zijian Liang, Yu-An Chen
Abstract: We study two-dimensional translation-invariant CSS stabilizer codes over prime-dimensional qudits on the square lattice under twisted boundary conditions, generalizing the Kitaev $\mathbb{Z}_p$ toric code by augmenting each stabilizer with two additional qudits. Using the Laurent-polynomial formalism, we adapt the Gr\"obner basis to compute the logical dimension $k$ efficiently, without explicitly constructing large parity-check matrices. We then perform a systematic search over various stabilizer realizations and lattice geometries for $p\in\{3,5,7,11\}$, identifying qudit low-density parity-check codes with the optimal finite-size performance. Representative examples include $[[242,10, \leq 22]]_3$ and $[[120,6, \leq 20]]_{11}$, both achieving $k d^{2}/n \leq 20$. Across the searched regime, the best observed $k d^{2}$ at fixed $n$ increases with $p$, with an empirical relation $k d^{2} = 0.0541 \, n^{2}\ln p + 3.84 \, n$, compatible with a Bravyi--Poulin--Terhal-type tradeoff when the interaction range grows with system size.

### Paper [304]
Title: Noise-induced suppression of MPO truncation errors in noisy random circuits and Lindbladian dynamics
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20400
Authors: Zhi-Yuan Wei, Joel Rajakumar, Jon Nelson, Jens Eisert, Daniel Malz, Michael J. Gullans, Alexey V. Gorshkov
Abstract: We study how matrix product operator (MPO) truncation errors evolve when simulating two setups: (1) one-dimensional (1D) Haar-random circuits under either depolarizing noise or amplitude-damping noise, and (2) 1D Lindbladian dynamics of a non-integrable quantum Ising model under either depolarizing or amplitude-damping noise. We first show that the average purity of the system density matrix relaxes to a steady value on a timescale that scales inversely with the noise rate. We find that truncation errors generated at earlier steps are approximately exponentially contracted by the subsequent noisy evolution, both with system size $N$ and with the evolution depth $t$, while the cumulative error results from the competition between this contraction and newly injected compression errors. We complement the local-circuit numerics with a solvable global random-unitary model in which the same channel-dependent factor governs the mean-squared contraction of traceless errors and transient mean purity. Its exact finite-$N$ results capture the weak-noise contraction rates and the non-unital stationary-purity trend. Combining the resulting $L_2$ error characterization with the observed late-time $L_1/L_2$ conversion yields an empirical estimate of the $L_1$ truncation error that can be exponentially tighter in $N$ than the generic rank-based bound, particularly for non-unital noise. Together, these results provide empirical evidence that MPO simulation algorithms may efficiently sample from the output of 1D noisy random circuits [setup (1)] with an arbitrary number of circuit layers, and from the steady state of 1D Lindbladian dynamics [setup (2)].

### Paper [305]
Title: Electrospinning-Data.org, a platform for electrospinning data and analysis
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27841
Authors: Mehrab Mahdian, Ferenc Ender, Tamas Pardy
Abstract: Electrospinning produces nanofibers by depositing a polymer solution in an electrostatic field. Fiber morphology is governed by interacting material, solution, process, hardware, and environmental conditions, requiring extensive tuning. Yet observations remain dispersed across the literature in heterogeneous forms that hinder aggregation and reuse. We present Electrospinning-Data.org, the first open, community-oriented research infrastructure dedicated to electrospinning experimental data. Its domain-specific data model links materials, solution properties, process and hardware configurations, environmental conditions, and fiber outcomes, including unstable and unsuccessful experiments. Numeric quantities are harmonized to canonical units and morphology is represented through controlled descriptors. The platform supports guided web, batch, and programmatic contributions, governed by automated structural validation and expert moderation. Its continuously evolving data are complemented by immutable DOI-bearing releases that support reproducible analyses. Public web interfaces, a REST API, and a Python client enable interactive and programmatic access. The integrated Virtual Lab supports exploratory analysis, machine-learning modeling, model interpretation, and evidence-linked parameter exploration. Together, these components establish a failure-aware foundation for reproducible, data-centric electrospinning research.

### Paper [306]
Title: QT-Net: Rethinking Evaluation of AI Models in Atomic Chemical Space
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10458
Authors: Pablo Mart\'inez Crespo, Stefano Ribes, Richard Beckmann, Marisa Gliege, Santiago Miret, Robert S. Jordan, Vijay Kris Narasimhan, Martin Rahm, Roc\'io Mercado
Abstract: Atomic properties such as partial charges or multipoles encode chemically meaningful information that can inform downstream molecular property prediction, but their evaluation as machine learning targets has been complicated by the absence of a principled out-of-distribution evaluation protocol at the atomic level. In this work, we propose a held-out evaluation protocol that clusters atomic environments by SOAP descriptors and computes metrics accounting only for cluster labels unseen during training. Following this procedure, we use 5$\times$5 cross-validation and Tukey's HSD to run a statistically rigorous comparison of E(3)-equivariant against non-equivariant, rotationally augmented models for predicting electron populations and multipoles of H, C, N, and O atoms. Building on our results, we introduce the Quantum Topological Neural Network (QT-Net), a rotationally augmented, non-equivariant graph neural network. We show that QT-Net can be used to infer properties of atoms in molecules from QM9 outside our training set, and that the atomic environments seen during training strongly affect the performance of molecular property prediction tasks. To further validate the framework, molecular dipole moments computed from QT-Net's per-atom outputs recover the ground-truth values reported in QM9. We release all code and data, including a JAX implementation of QT-Net.

### Paper [307]
Title: Composing diffusion priors with explicit physical context via generative Gibbs sampling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10642
Authors: Weizhou Wang, Jonathan Weare, Aaron R. Dinner
Abstract: Pretrained diffusion models provide powerful learned priors, but in scientific sampling the target distribution often depends on physical context that a single generative model can neither fully represent nor learn from scarce joint-system data. We introduce Generative Gibbs for Physics-Aware Sampling (GG-PA), a training-free framework that composes learned partial priors with explicit physical context through Gibbs sampling. We prove that this sampler is asymptotically exact as the diffusion time vanishes and remains exact at finite diffusion time for quadratic interactions. As a special case, the finite-time result corrects a finite-noise bias in plug-and-play split Gibbs samplers for linear inverse problems at no additional cost. We further introduce replica exchange over diffusion time to accelerate mixing. In experiments, a one-dimensional double-well prior recovers a context-induced distribution shift when coupled to an environment, and copies of such a prior composed on a lattice reproduce the emergent collective behavior of the $\phi^4$ model. In atomistic peptide systems, GG-PA captures a context-induced distribution shift and the spontaneous formation of hydrogen-bonded dimers from two copies of a prior trained only on the monomer.

### Paper [308]
Title: Collective excitations in quantum gravity condensates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18977
Authors: Andrea Calcinari, Adri\`a Delhom, Daniele Oriti
Abstract: A central open problem in quantum gravity is to understand how continuum spacetime emerges from quantum-geometric degrees of freedom in a background-independent setting. A many-body perspective suggests that spacetime emerges as a hydrodynamic phase of many atoms of quantum geometry. This idea underlies several approaches to quantum gravity, and it has been explicitly realised in the group field theory formalism. However, quantum fluctuations beyond the mean-field regime remain largely unexplored. We fill this gap by importing Bogolyubov theory to quantum gravity condensates, showing that leading beyond-mean-field effects manifest as collective excitations, in direct analogy with phonons in laboratory Bose-Einstein condensates. We implement the construction in a tractable group field theory model, where condensates of quantum-geometric atoms reproduce nonsingular expanding cosmologies, and derive the leading beyond-mean-field corrections to the emergent Friedmann dynamics. These results identify a new class of quantum-gravity excitations and establish a controlled bridge between microscopic quantum-gravitational dynamics, many-body collective phenomena, and signatures of spacetime emergence.

### Paper [309]
Title: Instabilities of the continuous superradiant laser
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21675
Authors: Bruno Laburthe-Tolra, Martin Robert-de-Saint-Vincent, Benjamin Pasquiou
Abstract: We investigate the intensity stability of the superradiant laser. Our study focuses on the architecture where a continuous beam of atoms in an electronically excited state crosses the mode of a high-finesse Fabry-Perot cavity, which has been proposed as a new architecture of an active optical clock. We show that such superradiant laser can become unstable and develop chaotic behavior. We derive an analytical criterion for this instability and find that it may only occur when the lifetime of photons in the cavity is significantly shorter than the lifetime of atoms. This criterion allows for refining the necessary parameters to run a superradiant laser as a frequency reference in the optical domain. In particular, we point-out the consequences of the instability on intensity fluctuations and laser linewidth. On the other hand, we also point out that the superradiant laser, when in the unstable regime, can become an interesting playground for studying chaos. At the mean-field level, there is a direct mapping to the B\'enard instability associated with fluid turbulence; however quantum fluctuations associated with photon out-coupling and atom re-filling substantially modify the expected behaviors. Finally, we point-out the existence of a regular self-pulsing regime at large atom numbers.

### Paper [310]
Title: Spin Textures and Eigenstate Evolution of Isospectrally Patterned Lattices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07502
Authors: Peter Schmelcher
Abstract: Isospectrally patterned lattices exhibit a composite band structure with a tunable ratio of localized versus delocalized eigenstates that is controlled by the underlying phase gradient. We show that the lattice Hamiltonian can be interpreted as that of a single spin which, exposed to a rotating magnetic field, hops across the lattice with a spin flip. In the low- and high-energy part of the band, the localized states show an envelope of oscillatory character separated by quasi-nodes. Spin peaks occur at the locations of these quasi-nodes and provide a unique spin texture to the eigenstates, which becomes increasingly complex with increasing degree of excitation. The crossover from localization to delocalization and vice versa leaves its fingerprints in the Fourier spectrum of the eigenstates: the original bimodal frequency distribution widens with increasing degree of excitation, moves across the spectral window, and finally culminates in an extremely narrow frequency peak. In the course of this evolution, the spin texture undergoes a rearrangement transition involving different characteristic (ir)regular patterns which we quantify by considering the total variation of the local spin fluctuations. Our results demonstrate the variety of the spectral properties of isospectrally patterned lattices, which holds great prospect, in particular when considering higher lattice or cell dimensions.

### Paper [311]
Title: Local Search with Correlated Randomness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17469
Authors: Yunbei Xu
Abstract: The running-time distribution of a randomized algorithm is computed under independent randomness, but independence is rarely what a source guarantees. We study sources $\nu$ with $\nu[w]\le DP[w]^s$ for every finite prefix $w$ of the tape, where $P$ is an independent reference law, $0<s\le1$, and $D\ge1$; individual choices may be predictable, even determined by the past. The worst-case probability of any closed tape event $E$ is exactly $\min\{1,DC_s(E)\}$, where $C_s(E)$ is the infimum cost of covering $E$ by prefixes of varying depth. This tree max-flow/min-cut identity makes worst-case probabilities exact and optimizable.
  Three applications follow. In scheduling, $n$ tasks on one machine are each retried until the next random symbol lies in its success set; every schedule has the same makespan distribution under independent randomness, yet under our constraint two fixed orders miss a linear deadline with worst-case probabilities $1$ and $\exp[-\Theta(n)]$, and a static priority rule is optimal at every deadline.
